On Maximal Delay of Stability Loss for Dynamical Bifurcations
Anatoly Neishtadt

Abstract
We consider a dynamical bifurcation caused by a slow passage through a static bifurcation point: in a system depending on a parameter, the parameter changes slowly in time and passes through the critical value corresponding to the loss of stability of an equilibrium via a Poincaré–Andronov–Hopf bifurcation in the frozen system. If the system is analytic, then the loss of stability is inevitably delayed: phase points attracted to the equilibrium in the stability region remain near the equilibrium for a long time after entering the instability region, so that the parameter changes by an amount of order 1 independently of how slow the variation of the parameter is. Remarkably, there exists a maximal delay : all phase points attracted to the stable equilibrium before a certain threshold value of the parameter leave a neighbourhood of the unstable equilibrium almost simultaneously near another threshold value of the parameter, known as a buffer point . A delay of stability loss beyond the buffer point is impossible unless the initial data have a very special form. We assume that, although the equilibrium is non-degenerate for real values of the parameter, one of its eigenvalues vanishes generically for some complex value of the parameter (a complex analogue of a saddle-node bifurcation), and that this complex singularity is, in a suitable sense, the closest one to the real Poincaré–Andronov–Hopf bifurcation point. We show that the value of maximal delay is determined by this complex singularity: the threshold values defining the maximal delay are the intersection points of the Stokes lines associated with this singularity and the real axis. We study these phenomena in the framework of slow–fast dynamical systems.



1
Introduction

There is a vast variety of applied problems leading to study of dynamical systems with phase variables changing on different time scales (
slow-fast dynamical systems
with
slow and fast variables
).
Stability loss delay
is a remarkable feature of dynamical bifurcations in such systems. Existence of maximal delay (known as
a buffer point
) is an important phenomenon in stability loss delay that is related to singularities of solutions in complex time in a way which is still to be understood. In this paper we provide an asymptotic description of the maximal delay in stability loss phenomenon at slow passage through a bifurcation in a generic setting.

Finite dimensional slow-fast dynamical systems with continuous time are described by ODEs

\displaystyle\frac{dx}{dt}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(x,\kappa,\varepsilon),\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

(1.1)

\displaystyle\frac{d\kappa}{dt}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon g(x,\kappa,\varepsilon),\quad\kappa\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.

Here
x
and
\kappa
are
fast
and
slow
variables. Small positive parameter
\varepsilon
characterises ratio of time scales of these variables. The first equation in (
1.1
) for

\kappa={\rm const}
and
\varepsilon=0
is called
the fast system
.

Let
x=X(\kappa)
be a non-degenerate equilibrium of the fast system:

f(X(\kappa),\kappa,0)\equiv 0
and eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}(\kappa),\lambda_{2}(\kappa),\ldots,\lambda_{n}(\kappa)
of this equilibrium are different from 0. Dynamics of slow variables near this equilibrium is approximately described by
the slow system

\frac{d\kappa}{dt}=\varepsilon g(X(\kappa),\kappa,0),\quad x=X(\kappa).

(1.2)

Assume that in the domain of slow variables there exists a bifurcation surface that separates regions of asymptotic stability and instability of this equilibrium. We say that there is a dynamical bifurcation with stability loss if drift of slow variables in (
1.2
) leads to crossing the bifurcation surface from the stability to instability region. Typically, two complex-conjugate eigenvalues cross imaginary axis (a Poincaré-Andronov-Hopf bifurcation in the fast system). Consider phase points of (
1.1
) that are attracted to the equilibrium of fast system in the stability region at the distance of order 1 from the bifurcation surface. We say that there is
a delay of stability loss
if these phase points stay near the equilibrium in the instability region during the time of order
1/\varepsilon
.

The buffer point phenomenon consists of the following. Fix a solution
\kappa=\mathrm{K}(\tau),x=X(\mathrm{K}(\tau)),\tau=\varepsilon t
of the slow system. Let
\tau_{*}
be a bifurcation value of
the slow time

\tau
, i.e.
\mathrm{K}(\tau)
crosses the bifurcation surface at
\tau=\tau_{*}
. The value
\tau_{*}^{+}>\tau_{*}
is
a buffer point
if there exists a slow time moment

\tau_{-}<\tau_{*}
such that all the phase points of (
1.1
) attracted to a small neighbourhood of equilibrium
X(\mathrm{K}(\tau))
before time
\tau_{*}^{-}
will leave a neighbourhood of the equilibrium near
\tau=\tau_{*}^{+}
. Thus, stability delay beyond time

\tau_{*}^{+}
is not possible for these phase points. Value
\tau_{*}^{+}-\tau_{*}
is
a maximal delay
. Phase points of (
1.1
) attracted to a small neighbourhood of equilibrium
X(\mathrm{K}(\tau))
at times
\tau
between
\tau_{*}^{-}
and
\tau_{*}
will leave a neighbourhood of equilibrium at times between
\tau_{*}
and
\tau_{*}^{+}
. Stability delay beyond time
\tau_{*}^{+}
occurs for phase points with special initial data. Those are phase points attracted to a small neighbourhood of equilibrium
X(\mathrm{K}(\tau))
close to the time
\tau_{*}^{-}
.

Theory of stability loss delay was started in Pontryagin’s school by Shishkova in
[
25
]
, where an example of this phenomenon in a non-trivial (
X(\mathrm{K}(\tau))\not\equiv{\rm const}
) setting was studied using methods of the dynamics in complex time. Such an approach and its modifications turned out to be a powerful tool in analysis of this phenomenon.

Systematic development of a general theory of the stability loss delay phenomenon was started in
[
17
,
18
,
19
,
3
]
. It was shown that if the system is analytic, the stability loss delay necessarily occurs
[
17
,
18
]
. It turns out that the analyticity is a property responsible for the stability loss delay phenomenon. There are examples of systems of smoothness
C^{\infty}
for which stability loss does not delay: an escape from an equilibrium occurs inside an interval of slow time that shrinks to a bifurcation value as
\varepsilon\to 0

[
18
]
. Asymptotic expressions for delay time were obtained in
[
19
]
under certain conditions. Namely, the analytic continuation of slow system solutions along some paths in complex time plane was considered, and it was assumed that the family of these paths is separated from singularities. These complex time singularities are related to existence of a buffer point. From the viewpoint of motion in real time, the buffer point phenomenon is related to an exponentially small mismatch of bounded backward and forward in time solutions
[
7
]
.

Stability loss delay accompanies also dynamical bifurcations of periodic trajectories in analytic slow-fast ODEs and fixed points in analytic slow-fast maps
[
17
,
8
,
9
,
23
,
27
,
28
,
10
]
.
Study of stability loss delay for infinite dimensional dynamical systems is a subject of active research in
[
26
,
30
,
14
,
11
,
2
,
29
]
.

Stability loss delay appears in diverse applications, including mechanics, laser physics, chemistry, epidemiology, ecology, neural models, climate modelling, and biochemistry (see references in
[
24
]
).

Maximal delay/buffer point was for the first time considered (without using these names) in a particular example in
[
25
]
. In this example there is a complex slow time point
\tau_{c}
where an eigenvalue of an equilibrium vanishes. Points
\tau_{*}^{-}
and
\tau_{*}^{+}
turned out to be points of intersection of Stokes lines passing through
\tau_{c}
and the axis of real time. At first glance, the example in
[
25
]
looks as a typical one as it presents a normal form for Poincaré-Andronov-Hopf bifurcation with slowly time dependent parameters. However, in this example position of equilibrium remains an analytic function of complex slow time at
\tau_{c}
, while generically
\tau_{c}
should be a branch point of order 2 for this position (a complex counterpart of saddle-node bifurcation). It turns out that the example in
[
25
]
is a degenerate one: two coefficients in expansion near
\tau_{c}
vanish while generically they should have non-zero values. In this paper, we consider the generic case: an eigenvalue vanishes at
\tau_{c}
, and the equilibrium
X(\mathrm{K}(\tau))
of the fast system has a branch point of order 2 at
\tau_{c}
. We do this for slow-fast systems of general form (
1.1
). The result is that, similarly to
[
25
]
,
\tau_{*}^{-}
and
\tau_{*}^{+}
are points of intersection of Stokes lines passing through
\tau_{c}
with the axis of real time. Values
\tau_{*}^{-}
and
\tau_{*}^{+}
describe maximal delay phenomenon in the limit as
\varepsilon\to 0
. We show that accuracy with which
\tau_{*}^{+}
provides the value of maximal delay is
O(\varepsilon\ln\varepsilon)
.

Our methodology is based on study of solutions of systems (
1.1
) in complex time. Let the solution
\kappa=\mathrm{K}(\tau),x=X(\mathrm{K}(\tau)),\tau=\varepsilon t
of the slow system (
1.2
) be defined on an interval

\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]\subset\mathbb{R}
. Let
\lambda_{1}(\mathrm{K}(\tau)),\lambda_{2}(\mathrm{K}(\tau))
be two complex conjugate eigenvalues of the equilibrium
X(\mathrm{K}(\tau))
of the fast system. Assume that real parts of these eigenvalues are negative at
\tau<\tau_{*}
, zero at
\tau=\tau_{*}
, and positive at
\tau>\tau_{*}
;
here
\tau_{*}\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]
is the bifurcation value of slow time. Other eigenvalues have negative real parts for
\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]
. Assuming analyticity of functions
f,g
in (
1.1
) with respect to
x,\kappa
, consider systems (
1.1
) , (
1.2
) for complex values of phase variables and time. In the complex time plane consider a path
\tau=\Gamma(s)
parametrised by a real variable
s
. Take
s/\varepsilon
as a new time for the fast system. Then eigenvalues of the equilibrium
X(\mathrm{K}(\Gamma(s)))
are
\Lambda_{j}(s)=\Gamma^{\prime}(s)\lambda_{j}(\mathrm{K}(\Gamma(s))),j=1,2,\ldots,n
. Let us call path
\tau=\Gamma(s)

iso-expanding
1
1

1

This name is suggested by Prof Carles Simo.
, if
\Lambda_{1}(s)
is an imaginary number. For motion in real time
t
, initially there is a contraction in all fast variables (for
\varepsilon t<\tau_{*}
) and then an expansion in two fast variables (for
\varepsilon t>\tau_{*}
). For motion along an iso-expanding path there is neither contraction nor expansion in one of fast variables in the linearised system. There is a smooth family of iso-expanding paths that connect points of stable (
\varepsilon t<\tau_{*}
) and unstable (
\varepsilon t>\tau_{*}
) parts of the real time axis near
\tau_{*}
. Study of dynamics when time changes along such a path (see
[
19
]
) provides a proof of existence of stability loss delay and asymptotic formulas for time of delay. Points of the real time axis connected by such a path are (asymptotically) moments of time of attraction to the equilibrium
X(\mathrm{K}(\tau))
and of escape from it. Continuation of iso-expanding paths family away from
\tau_{*}
in the domain of analyticity of functions
f,g
is prevented by singularities of paths. Typical singularities are related to a zero eigenvalue or a double eigenvalue:
\lambda_{1}(\mathrm{K}(\tau_{c}))=0
, or

\lambda_{1}(\mathrm{K}(\tau_{c}))=\lambda_{2}(\mathrm{K}(\tau_{c}))
at some complex point
\tau_{c}
. In this paper we consider the case of a zero eigenvalue. Iso-expanding path through
\tau_{c}
is responsible for the buffer point phenomenon.

Generically,
\tau_{c}
is a branch point of order 2 for
X(\mathrm{K}(\tau))
. Dynamics near
\tau_{c}
is approximately described by a complex Riccati equation whose real counterpart plays a fundamental role in relaxation oscillations theory
[
16
]
. In complex time this equation is considered in
[
5
]
. Its role for determining maximal is discussed in
[
21
]
. We study an effect of this dynamics upon the real time dynamics. This is achieved via analytic continuation of solutions along iso-expanding paths. To avoid growing norm solutions we will use the following approach (see
[
19
]
): complex system of
n+m
differential equations is replaced by a system of
n+m-1
equations with a complex non-constant delay using the real-analyticity conditions. In study of analytic continuation we use a perturbative approach. Outside
\sim\varepsilon^{2/3}
- neighbourhood of
\tau_{c}
this is a perturbation relative to solutions of the linearised near the equilibrium equation.
In this neighbourhood this is a perturbation relative to solutions of the Riccati equation. Choice of
\sim\varepsilon^{2/3}
- neighbourhood of
\tau_{c}
is determined by the condition that both perturbative processes provide the same accuracy at the boundary of this neighbourhood. On the boundary of this neighbourhood one should switch from an iso-expanding path determined by linearisation near equilibria of the fast system to an iso-expanding path determined by the Riccati equation. Inside
\sim\varepsilon^{2/3}
- neighbourhood of
\tau_{c}
we consider perturbations near two special solutions of the Riccati equation. A special solution of the Riccati equation is a solution that approaches an equilibrium of the fast system constructed for the Riccati equation as time changes along an iso-expanding path through
\tau_{c}
. We switch from one special solution to another at
\tau_{c}
. This switch is a cornerstone of the methodology. At this switch, at
\tau=\tau_{c}
, the phase point of the original system (
1.1
) turned out to be at a distance
\sim\varepsilon^{1/3}
from a special solution of the Riccati equation. As a result, for moving along an appropriate iso-expanding path
\Gamma_{+,\varepsilon}
, far from
\tau_{c}
, this phase points remains at a distance
\sim\varepsilon^{1/3}
from the equilibrium of the fast system. It keeps this distance up to the point
\tau_{+,\varepsilon}
of intersection of this iso-expanding path with the axis of real time. At the slow time moment
\tau_{+,\varepsilon}
the phase point is in the instability region:
\operatorname{Re}\lambda_{1,2}(\mathrm{K}(\tau_{+,\varepsilon}))>{\rm const}>0
. Thus, during the real slow time interval of length
\sim\varepsilon\ln\varepsilon
centred at
\tau_{+,\varepsilon}
the distance of the phase point from the equilibrium of the fast system grows from a value
\sim\varepsilon^{1/3}
to a value
\sim 1
. Therefore,

\tau_{*}^{+}=\tau_{+,\varepsilon}+O(\varepsilon\ln\varepsilon)
. This constitutes the principal result of the paper: Theorem
1
in Section
3
.

2
Formulation of conditions

The natural framework for dynamical bifurcation theory is that of systems with
slow and fast variables (slow-fast systems) (
1.1
). Here
(x,\kappa)\in D\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+m}
, and

|\varepsilon|<\varepsilon_{1}={\rm const}
. The fast system is the first equation in
(
1.1
) with
\kappa={\rm const}
and
\varepsilon=0
.
We suppose that for all
\kappa

in the projection of
D
onto the
\kappa
-space the fast system has an
equilibrium position
x=X(\kappa)
depending continuously on
\kappa
. Let

\lambda_{i}(\kappa)
,
i=1,2,...n
, be eigenvalues of this equilibrium. For the
slow system
(
1.2
)
we fix a solution
\kappa=\mathrm{K}(\tau)
,
x=X(\mathrm{K}(\tau))
with the slow time
\tau=\varepsilon t\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]
,
\varepsilon>0
, and consider behavior of
the eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}(\kappa)
,
i=1,2,...n
along it. Let
\lambda_{1}(\mathrm{K}(\tau))
be in the left complex
half-plane for
\tau<\tau_{*}\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]
, and cross
imaginary axis for
\tau=\tau_{*}
with a nonzero velocity. Let

\lambda_{1}(\mathrm{K}(\tau_{*}))\neq 0
, and
\lambda_{2}(\mathrm{K}(\tau_{*}))=\overline{\lambda_{1}(\mathrm{K}(\tau_{*}))}
(the bar here and below indicates
complex conjugation). Let
\lambda_{i}((\mathrm{K}(\tau))
,
i=3,\ldots,n
, be in the
left complex half-plane for all
\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]
. We assume that the right-hand sides
in (
1.1
) can be continued analytically with respect
to
x
,
\kappa
and smoothly in
\varepsilon
into a complex neighbourhood
of the point

(X(\mathrm{K}(\tau_{*}))
,
\mathrm{K}(\tau_{*}))
, and this neighbourhood does not depend on
\varepsilon
.
Then
there is a stability loss delay in system (
1.1
)

[
17
,
18
]
.

To formulate conditions of the main theorem of this paper we use constructions
related to analytic continuation of the slow solution
X,\mathrm{K}
.
We may assume that
{\rm Im}\,\lambda_{1}(\mathrm{K}(\tau_{*}))<0
. For

\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]
, let us introduce the complex phase
\Psi(\tau)=\int\limits_{\tau_{*}}^{\tau}\lambda_{1}(\mathrm{K}(\vartheta))d\vartheta
. The function
{\rm Re}\,\Psi
has a minimum at
\tau=\tau_{*}

since at this point

{\rm Re}\,\lambda_{1}(\mathrm{K}(\tau))
changes sign from negative to positive.
Let
\Pi
be the function that maps
a slow time moment
\tau_{-}<\tau_{*}
into the slow time moment

\Pi(\tau_{-})>\tau_{*}
such that
{\rm Re}\,\Psi(\tau_{-})={\rm Re}\,\Psi(\Pi(\tau_{-}))
. The function
\Pi
is well defined on an interval

[\hat{\tau},\tau_{*}],\quad\hat{\tau}<\tau_{*}
. The slow solution
can be analytically continued
into some neighbourhood of the point
\tau_{*}
in the plane of the
complex slow time
\tau
. The function
\Psi
can be continued into
the same neighbourhood. Points
\tau_{-}

and
\Pi(\tau_{-})
are connected by an arc
\Gamma_{\tau_{-}}
of the
level curve
{\rm Re}\,\Psi={\rm const}
in the upper half-plane of complex slow time. If
\tau_{-}
is
sufficiently close to
\tau_{*}
, then in the domain
K_{\tau_{-}}

bounded by
\Gamma_{\tau_{-}}
and its complex conjugate

{\overline{\Gamma}}_{\tau_{-}}
(Fig.
1
) the following conditions are
satisfied: 1) the slow solution
X,\,\mathrm{K}
is analytic; 2) the
right-hand sides of (
1.1
) are analytic at the points of
the slow solution; 3)
\lambda_{1,2}(\mathrm{K})\neq 0
;
4)
\lambda_{1}(\mathrm{K})\neq\lambda_{2}(\mathrm{K})
,
\lambda_{i}(\mathrm{K})\neq\lambda_{j}(\mathrm{K}),\quad i=1,2,\quad j=3,\ldots,n
; 5)
{\rm Re}\,\lambda_{j}(\mathrm{K})<0,\quad j=3,\ldots,n
, for real
\tau
, and the linearized near the
equilibrium fast system considered along any curve

{\rm Re}\,\Psi(\tau)={\rm const}
has
(n-2)
eigenvalues with
negative real parts corresponding to the eigenvalues

\lambda_{3},\ldots,\lambda_{n}
;
6) the tangent lines to the curves
\operatorname{Re}\Psi={\rm const}
are not vertical.

Figure 1:
Curves in the complex slow time plane

Let
\tau_{*}^{-}
be the lower bound
of the values
\tau_{-}
for which conditions 1) - 6) hold for

K_{\tau_{-}}
. Denote

\tau_{*}^{+}=\sup_{\tau_{*}^{-}<\tau<\tau_{*}}\Pi(\tau),\quad K_{*}=\bigcup_{\tau_{*}^{-}<\tau_{-}<\tau_{*}}K_{\tau_{-}}.\quad

Remark.
Condition 6) in the definition of
\tau_{*}^{-}
can be relaxed. It is sufficient to require that there exists a family of nonintersecting symmetric with respect to the real axis smooth curves which are transversal to curves
\operatorname{Re}\Psi={\rm const}
, and this family covers
K_{\tau_{-}}
.

On the boundary of the region
K_{*}
one of the conditions 1) - 6)
should be violated. We will suppose, that this is condition
3), i.e.
\lambda_{1}=0
at some point
\tau_{c}
of the
boundary of
K_{*}
in the upper half-plane,
and then
\lambda_{2}=0
at the complex conjugate point
\bar{\tau}_{c}
. We will assume also that the
right hand-sides of the system (
1.1
) are
in a general position in a neighbourhood of the point
X(\mathrm{K}(\tau_{c})),\mathrm{K}(\tau_{c})
. More
precisely, we suppose that the following assumptions A - E
and general position conditions F, G are satisfied.

A. Values
\tau_{*}^{-},\tau_{*}^{+}
are finite.

B. The component
\Gamma_{*}
of the
\partial K_{*}
in the upper half-plane
is a connected curve. Denote
\bar{\Gamma}_{*}
the complex
conjugate to
\Gamma_{*}
.

C. The slow solution is continuous on
\partial K_{*}
.

D. There is a unique point
\tau_{c}\in\Gamma_{*}
such that

\lambda_{1}(\mathrm{K}(\tau_{c}))=0
. We assume that
\lambda_{j}(\mathrm{K}(\tau_{c}))\neq 0,\,j=2,\ldots,n.

(Then
\lambda_{2}(\mathrm{K}(\bar{\tau}_{c}))=0
,
\lambda_{j}(\mathrm{K}(\bar{\tau}_{c}))\neq 0,\,j=1,3,\ldots,n.
)

E. Conditions 2, 4 - 6 are satisfied in the domain
K_{*}\cup\partial K_{*}
.

Denote

\kappa_{c}=\mathrm{K}(\tau_{c}),\quad x_{c}=X(\kappa_{c}),\quad A_{c}=\partial f(x_{c},\kappa_{c},0)/\partial x.

According to condition D,
matrix
A_{c}
has one zero eigenvalue.
Let
z_{1}
be the projection of the vector
(x-x_{c})
onto the
eigenvector of the matrix
A_{c}
which
corresponds to this eigenvalue. Let
z_{2}
be the projection
of the vector
(x-x_{c})
onto the eigenspace of the matrix
A_{c}
which
corresponds to all other eigenvalues. Then

\dot{z}_{1}=b\cdot(\kappa-\kappa_{c})+az_{1}^{2}+O(\varepsilon+|z_{1}|^{3}+|\kappa-\kappa_{c}|(|z_{1}|+|z_{2}|)+|z_{1}||z_{2}|+|z_{2}|^{2}+|\kappa-\kappa_{c}|^{2}).

(2.1)

Here and below, norm
|\cdot|
is the sum of absolute values of vector’s components.

We will consider the problem under the following conditions of general position:

F.
a\neq 0
.

G.
b\cdot g(x_{c},\kappa_{c},0)\neq 0
.

Remarks.

1. Problems in
[
25
,
15
,
7
]
have
a=0
,
b=0
.

2. Under conditions F, G, the equilibrium
X(\mathrm{K}(\tau))
has second-order
branching at the point
\tau_{c}
.

3. Level curves
{\rm Re}\,\Psi={\rm const}
passing through the point

\tau_{c}
are called the Stokes lines. The asymptotic
behavior
of solutions of the system (
1.1
) near point
\tau_{c}
is
changing abruptly across these lines.

In the proof of Theorem
1
below we will use one more condition denoted as H. This condition simplify the reasonings, but the theorem is valid without this condition.

Clearly

\lambda_{1}(\mathrm{K}(\tau))\neq 2\lambda_{2}(\mathrm{K}(\tau)),\lambda_{2}(\mathrm{K}(\tau))\neq 2\lambda_{1}(\mathrm{K}(\tau))

(2.2)

for
\tau
close to
\tau_{c},\bar{\tau}_{c}
and for real
\tau
. We will assume that

H). Condition (
2.2
) is satisfied for
\tau\in K_{*}\cup\partial K_{*}
.

There will be a comment in the proof about an addition to the proof required to avoid using the condition H.

For simplicity in the formulations of intermediate results we will assume the following:
\operatorname{Re}\tau_{c}=\operatorname{Re}\tau_{*}
; along the curves
\operatorname{Re}\Psi(\tau)={\rm const}
the function
\operatorname{Im}\tau
attains a unique maximum, and this maximum occurs at

.

The curve

at an angle of

. We will denote them as
\Gamma_{*,1}
(the left one) and

\Gamma_{*,2}

3
Time of stability loss delay

Let
(x(t),\kappa(t))
be the phase point of the system (
1.1
)
with initial condition
\kappa(t_{0})=\kappa_{0}=\mathrm{K}(\tau_{0})
,
t_{0}=\tau_{0}/\varepsilon
.

Theorem 1

If
\tau_{0}<\tau_{*}^{-}-C_{1}\varepsilon|\ln\varepsilon|
and the initial point

(x(t_{0}),\kappa(t_{0}))
is in
C_{2}^{-1}
-neighbourhood of the equilibrium

(X(\kappa_{0}),\kappa_{0})
, then

a) for
\tau_{0}+C_{1}\varepsilon|\ln\varepsilon|\leq\varepsilon t\leq\tau_{*}^{+}-C_{3}\varepsilon|\ln\varepsilon|

the phase point
(x(t),\kappa(t))
is in a
C_{4}\varepsilon
-neighbourhood of the equilibrium

(X(\mathrm{K}(\varepsilon t)),\mathrm{K}(\varepsilon t))
,

b) there exists
t_{d}
such that
|\varepsilon t_{d}-\tau_{*}^{+}|<C_{3}\varepsilon|\ln\varepsilon|

and
|x(t_{d})-X(\kappa(t_{d}))|>C_{5}^{-1}
.

Here and henceforth
C_{i},c_{i},k_{i}
are positive constants. The appearance of
C_{i}
in some relation is equivalent to the assertion
that there exists
C_{i}
satisfying this relation for small enough
\varepsilon>0
(and
similarly for other constants).

According to Theorem
1
all the phase points which were
attracted to the slow solution before the moment of slow time

\tau_{*}^{-}
will depart from the slow solution in
O(\varepsilon|\ln\varepsilon|)
-neighbourhood
of the moment of slow time
\tau_{*}^{+}
. Note that the last statement of Theorem
1
implies
|x(t_{d})-X(\mathrm{K}(\varepsilon t_{d}))|>C_{6}^{-1}
. (Indeed,
\dot{\kappa}=O(\varepsilon),\dot{\mathrm{K}}=O(\varepsilon)
. Thus,
\kappa(t_{d})-\mathrm{K}(\varepsilon t_{d})=O(\varepsilon\ln\varepsilon)
.)

The proof is based on analytic continuation of the solution in a certain domain
in the complex slow time plane. This domain is close to the domain
K_{*}
. Outside

O(\varepsilon^{2/3})
-neighbourhoods of the points
\tau_{c}
,
\bar{\tau}_{c}
in this
domain, the motion can be described by perturbation theory as a perturbation
of the slow solution. In
O(\varepsilon^{2/3})
-neighbourhoods of the points
\tau_{c}
,
\bar{\tau}_{c}
the system can be considered as a perturbation of some
auxiliary Riccati equation. This equation (for real time) plays an important
role in the theory of relaxation oscillations, and its solution can be
expressed via Bessel functions. We will consider this equation in
Section
4.2
.

Remark.
The phase points which were attracted to the slow solution near the moment of the slow time

\tau_{0}\in(\tau_{*}^{-},\tau_{*})
will depart from the slow solution near the moment of the slow time
\Pi(\tau_{0})

[
17
,
19
]
.

Theorem
1
was announced in
[
21
]
. Numerical demonstration of maximal delay phenomenon in situation of Theorem
1
is contained in
[
22
]
.

4
Auxiliary constructions

4.1
Some curves in the plane of the slow time

Recall that the curve
\Gamma_{*}
consists of two smooth components which meet at
\tau_{c}
at an angle of
120^{\circ}
. They are denoted as
\Gamma_{*,1}
(the left one) and
\Gamma_{*,2}
(the right one).

Now, consider the slow-fast system obtained by expanding and truncating the original system near the point

(X(\kappa_{c}),\kappa_{c})
:

\displaystyle\dot{z}_{1}=b\cdot(\kappa-\kappa_{c})+az_{1}^{2},

(4.1)

\displaystyle\dot{\kappa}=\varepsilon g_{c},\ g_{c}=g(x_{c},\kappa_{c},0).

(Recall that
z_{1}
is the projection of the vector
(x-x_{c})
onto the
eigenvector of the matrix
A_{c}
corresponding to zero eigenvalue.)
For
\kappa
, we consider the solution
\kappa=\mathrm{K}_{a}(\tau)=g_{c}(\tau-\tau_{c})+\kappa_{c}
.
Take the equilibrium of the fast system
{Z_{1}}_{a}(\kappa)=\sqrt{-a^{-1}b\cdot(\kappa-\kappa_{c})}
. The eigenvalue of this equilibrium is
{\Lambda_{1}}_{a}(\kappa)=2a{Z_{1}}_{a}=2a\sqrt{-a^{-1}b\cdot(\kappa-\kappa_{c})}
. We should choose an appropriate branch of the square root to have an agreement between
X(\mathrm{K}(\tau))
and
{Z_{1}}_{a}(\mathrm{K}_{a}(\tau))
for

\tau
close to
\tau_{c}
in
K_{*}
.
This choice is described below.

Introduce the complex phase

\psi_{a}(\tau)=\int_{\tau_{c}}^{\tau}{\Lambda_{1}}_{a}(\mathrm{K}_{a}(\vartheta))\,d\vartheta=\int_{\tau_{c}}^{\tau}2a\sqrt{-a^{-1}b\cdot g_{c}(\vartheta-\tau_{c})}\,d\vartheta=\frac{4}{3}a\sqrt{-a^{-1}b\cdot g_{c}(\tau-\tau_{c})^{3}}.

(4.2)

The set
\{\tau\,:\,{\rm Re}\,\psi_{a}(\tau)=0\}
consists of three rays that meet at
\tau_{c}
(Figure
2
).

Figure 2:
Complex rays

Denote by

\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
the rays that are tangent

2
2

2

The order of tangency is
1/2
, see Lemma
4.1
.
, respectively, to

\Gamma_{*,1}
and
\Gamma_{*,2}
(Fig.
3
). We choose the branch of the square root in the definition of
{Z_{1}}_{a}(\kappa)
such that the sign of
{\rm Re}\,\psi_{a}(\tau)
in the domain between
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
is the same as the sign of
{\rm Re}\,(\Psi(\tau)-\Psi(\tau_{c}))
in the domain

K_{*}
. This choice implies that
\operatorname{Re}\psi_{a}(\tau)<0
in the angle between
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
because
\operatorname{Re}\Psi(\tau)
has a minimum at
\tau=\tau_{*}
.

Lemma 4.1

Distance between points of
\Gamma_{*,j}
and
\gamma_{j}
measured along normals to
\Gamma_{*,j}
,

j=1,2
is
O(|\tau-\tau_{c}|^{3/2})
, where
\tau
is used as a parameter along
\Gamma_{*,j}
. The same estimate is valid if
\tau
is taken on
\gamma_{j}
and distance is measured along normals to
\gamma_{j}
.

Figure 3:
Curves
\Gamma_{1,*},\Gamma_{2,*}
and rays
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}

Consider also a curve
\Gamma_{\tau_{-}}
with
\tau_{-}
close to
\tau_{*}^{-}
(notation
\Gamma_{\tau_{-}}
is introduced in Section
2
).

Lemma 4.2

Distance between points of
\Gamma_{\tau_{-}}
and
\Gamma_{*,1}
measured along normals to
\Gamma_{*,1}
is
of order
|\tau_{-}-\tau_{*}^{-}|/\sqrt{|\tau-\tau_{c}|}
for
\tau
such that
|\tau-\tau_{c}|>{\rm const}|\tau_{-}-\tau_{*}^{-}|^{2/3}
, where
\tau
is the parameter along
\Gamma_{*,1}
.

Take any point
\tau_{\gamma}
on
\gamma_{1}
close to
\tau_{c}
. Its distance from
\Gamma_{*,1}
is
O(|\tau_{\gamma}-\tau_{c}|^{3/2})
. Consider the curve
\operatorname{Re}\Psi(\tau)={\rm const}
passing through
\tau_{\gamma}
(i.e.
\operatorname{Re}\Psi(\tau)=\operatorname{Re}\Psi(\tau_{\gamma}))
. This curve crosses the real axis at some point
\tau_{-}
such that
|\tau_{-}-\tau_{*}^{-}|/\sqrt{|\tau_{\gamma}-\tau_{c}|}=O(|\tau_{\gamma}-\tau_{c}|^{3/2})
, i.e. at
|\tau_{-}-\tau_{*}^{-}|=O(|\tau_{\gamma}-\tau_{c}|^{2})
. These estimates used the assumption
|\tau_{\gamma}-\tau_{c}|>{\rm const}|\tau_{-}-\tau_{*}^{-}|^{2/3}
i.e.
|\tau_{-}-\tau_{*}^{-}|<{\rm const}^{-1}|\tau_{\gamma}-\tau_{c}|^{3/2}
, which is satisfied. Thus, if
|\tau_{\gamma}-\tau_{c}|\sim\varepsilon^{2/3}
, then
|\tau_{-}-\tau_{*}^{-}|=O(\varepsilon^{4/3})
.

Figure 4:
Points
\tau_{\gamma},\tau_{p},\tau_{q}

Consider in the
\tau
-plane the line through the point
\tau_{\gamma}
parallel to the real axis. Take any point
\tau_{p}
on this line with
\operatorname{Re}\tau_{\gamma}\leq\operatorname{Re}\tau_{p}\leq\operatorname{Re}\tau_{c}
(Fig.
4
). Denote by
\Gamma_{p}
the segment of the curve
\operatorname{Re}\Psi(\tau)={\rm const}
with
\operatorname{Re}\tau\leq\operatorname{Re}\tau_{p}
(assumptions made for simplicity of formulations at the end of Section
2
imply that
\tau_{p}
is the only intersection of this curve and the line
\operatorname{Im}\tau=\operatorname{Im}\tau_{\gamma}
at
\operatorname{Re}\tau\leq\operatorname{Re}\tau_{c}
). Denote by
\tau_{p,-}
the point of intersection of the curve
\Gamma_{p}
and the real axis.
Then
|\tau_{p,-}-\tau_{-}|\sim|\tau_{p}-\tau_{\gamma}|\sqrt{|\tau_{\gamma}-\tau_{c}|}=O(|\tau_{\gamma}-\tau_{c}|^{3/2})
. Thus, if
|\tau_{\gamma}-\tau_{c}|\sim\varepsilon^{2/3}
then
|\tau_{p,-}-\tau_{-}|=O(\varepsilon)
.

Consider now the vertical line through
\tau_{c}
. Let
\tau_{q}
be a point on this line below
\tau_{c}
and close to
\tau_{c}
(Fig.
4
). Denote by
\Gamma_{q}
the curve
\operatorname{Re}\Psi(\tau)={\rm const}
passing through
\tau_{q}
(i.e.
\operatorname{Re}\Psi(\tau)=\operatorname{Re}\Psi(\tau_{q}))
. This curve crosses the real axis at some points
\tau_{q,-}
(the left one) and
\tau_{q,+}
(the right one).

Lemma 4.3

Distance between points of
\Gamma_{q}
and
\Gamma_{*,1}\cup\Gamma_{*,2}
measured along normals to
\Gamma_{q}
is of order
|\tau_{q}-\tau_{c}|^{3/2}/\sqrt{|\tau-\tau_{c}|}
, where
\tau
is the parameter along
\Gamma_{q}
.

In particular,
|\tau_{q,\pm}-\tau_{*}^{\pm}|
is of order
|\tau_{q}-\tau_{c}|^{3/2}
. If
|\tau_{q}-\tau_{c}|\sim\varepsilon^{2/3}
then
|\tau_{q,\pm}-\tau_{*}^{\pm}|\sim\varepsilon
.

4.2
Riccati equation

Consider equations (
4.1
), and take
\kappa=\mathrm{K}_{a}(\tau)=g_{c}(\tau-\tau_{c})+\kappa_{c}
:

\frac{dz_{1}}{dt}=b\cdot g_{c}(\tau-\tau_{c})+az_{1}^{2}.

(4.3)

This Riccati equation is well known in the theory of
relaxation oscillations
[
16
]
.
For frozen value of
\tau
it has equilibria at
z_{1}=\sqrt{-a^{-1}b\cdot g_{c}(\tau-\tau_{c})}

(for
\tau\neq\tau_{c}
there are two equilibria corresponding to two values of the square root). The eigenvalues of these equilibria are
2a\sqrt{-a^{-1}b\cdot g_{c}(\tau-\tau_{c})}
. The complex phase introduced in Section
4.1
is

\psi_{a}(\tau)=\int_{\tau_{c}}^{\tau}2a\sqrt{-a^{-1}b\cdot g_{c}(\vartheta-\tau_{c})}\,d\vartheta=\frac{4}{3}a\sqrt{-a^{-1}b\cdot g_{c}(\tau-\tau_{c})^{3}}.

Let
a=u\exp(iw),\ -a^{-1}b\cdot g_{c}=p\exp(iq),\,\tau-\tau_{c}=r\exp(i\varphi)
with real
u,w,p,q,r,\varphi
;
u>0,p>0,r\geq 0
. (Notation
p,q
here should not be mixed with
p,q
in the previous Section.) Then

\psi_{a}(\tau)=\frac{4}{3}up^{1/2}r^{3/2}\exp{(i(3(\varphi+2\pi l)+q+2w)/2)},\,l=1,2,\ldots

The set
\{\tau\,:\,{\rm Re}\,\psi_{a}(\tau)=0\}
consists of three rays
3\varphi+q+2w=3\pi+2\pi k
, i.e.

\varphi=\varphi_{k}=\pi+(2\pi/3)k-q/3-2w/3,\ k=1,2,3
, that meet at
\tau_{c}
.
These rays where denoted
\gamma_{1,2.3}
in the previous Section. We assume that the rays
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
correspond, respectively, to
k=1
and
k=2
, i.e. to
\varphi_{1}=5\pi/3-q/3-2w/3
and

\varphi_{2}=7\pi/3-q/3-2w/3
3
3

3

One could have in mind a model example
\dot{z}=i(\tau-\tau_{c})+z^{2}
, where
q=3\pi/2,w=0,\varphi_{1}=7\pi/6,\varphi_{2}=11\pi/6
.
. At
\tau_{c}
, these rays are tangent
4
4

4

The order of tangency is
1/2
, see Lemma
4.1
.
, respectively, to
\Gamma_{*,1}
and
\Gamma_{*,2}
. Choice of the branch of the square root at the definition of the equilibrium and the complex phase is described in the previous Section. This choice is such that
\operatorname{Re}\psi_{a}(\tau)<0
in the
120^{\circ}
-angle between
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
. This way we have a unique equilibrium
z_{1}=z_{1,a}(\tau)
for
\tau
in this angle.

Introduce a new slow time
s
and a new dependent variable
\zeta
by formulas

\tau=\tau_{c}-s\exp(i\varphi_{1})
,
z_{1}=\exp(i\alpha_{1})\zeta,\ \alpha_{1}=-\pi/6+q/3-w/3
. The equation in the new variables takes the form

\varepsilon\frac{d\zeta}{ds}=i(|a|ps+|a|\zeta^{2}).

(4.4)

The ray
\gamma_{1}
corresponds to
s\leq 0
.
Introduce new variables
\hat{\zeta},\hat{s}
via formulas

\zeta=\varepsilon^{1/3}\frac{p^{1/3}}{|a|^{1/3}}\hat{\zeta},\ s=\varepsilon^{2/3}\frac{1}{p^{1/3}|a|^{2/3}}\hat{s}.

(4.5)

The equation for the new variables is

\frac{d\hat{\zeta}}{d\hat{s}}=i(\hat{s}+\hat{\zeta}^{2}).

(4.6)

This equation by replacing
\hat{\zeta}
with
i\hat{\zeta}
and
\hat{s}
with
-\hat{s}
is reduced to equation (9.2) in
[
16
]
, whose general solution is given by formula (9.6) in
[
16
]
. Returning to variables
\hat{\zeta},\hat{s}
we get that the general solution to
equation (
4.6
) is

\hat{\zeta}=-i\sqrt{-\hat{s}}\,\frac{J_{-2/3}(v)-RJ_{2/3}(v)}{RJ_{-1/3}(v)+J_{1/3}(v)},\quad v=\frac{2}{3}(-\hat{s})^{3/2}.

(4.7)

Here
J_{\nu}(\cdot)
is the Bessel function of the order
\nu
,
and
R
is a constant which should be defined by initial conditions.

Note that the phase
\psi_{a}
(
4.2
) is invariant with respect to transformations of time and dependent variable. Thus

\psi_{a}=-\frac{4}{3}i(-\hat{s})^{3/2}=-2iv.

(4.8)

We will use asymptotic expansions of Bessel functions for large and small values of
v

[
13
]
:

\displaystyle J_{\nu}(v)

\displaystyle=(\pi v/2)^{-1/2}\cos(v-\nu\pi/2-\pi/4)[1+O(|v|^{-2})]

(4.9)

\displaystyle-(\pi v/2)^{-1/2}\sin(v-\nu\pi/2-\pi/4)[\frac{1}{2v}(\nu^{2}-\frac{1}{4})+O(|v|^{-3})],\ -\pi<\arg v<\pi

and

J_{\nu}(v)=\frac{v^{\nu}}{2^{\nu}}\frac{1}{\Gamma(\nu+1)}[1+O(|v|^{2})].

(4.10)

Lemma 4.4

Asymptotic expansion (
4.9
) implies that

\hat{\zeta}=-\sqrt{-\hat{s}}[1+O(|v|^{-1})]\ {\rm for}\ \operatorname{Im}s=0,\ s\leq 0,\ R=R_{-}=\exp((2\pi i)/3).

(4.11)

Lemma 4.5

Asymptotic expansion (
4.10
) implies that

\hat{\zeta}=\frac{1}{R}\frac{-2\pi i}{\Gamma^{2}(1/3)3^{1/6}}[1+O(|v|^{2/3})].

(4.12)

Now, introduce in equations (
4.1
) a new
slow time
\sigma
and a new dependent variable
\chi
by formulas

\tau=\tau_{c}-\sigma\exp(i\varphi_{2}),\ z_{1}=\exp(i\alpha_{2})\chi
, where

\varphi_{2}=7\pi/3-q/3-2w/3,\ \alpha_{2}=-5\pi/6+q/3-w/3
. The equation in the new variables takes the same form
(
4.4
), but now for variables
\sigma,\chi
:

\varepsilon\frac{d\chi}{d\sigma}=i(|a|p\sigma+|a|\chi^{2}).

(4.13)

The ray
\gamma_{2}
corresponds to
\sigma\leq 0
.
Introduce new variables
\hat{\chi},\hat{\sigma}
via formulas

\chi=\varepsilon^{1/3}\frac{p^{1/3}}{|a|^{1/3}}\hat{\chi},\ \sigma=\varepsilon^{2/3}\frac{1}{p^{1/3}|a|^{2/3}}\hat{\sigma}.

(4.14)

The equation in the new variables takes the same form
(
4.6
), but now for variables
\hat{\sigma},\hat{\chi}
:

\frac{d\hat{\chi}}{d\hat{\sigma}}=i(\hat{\sigma}+\hat{\chi}^{2}).

(4.15)

The general solution to
this equation is again(
4.7
):

\hat{\chi}=-i\sqrt{-\hat{\sigma}}\,\frac{J_{-2/3}(v)-RJ_{2/3}(v)}{RJ_{-1/3}(v)+J_{1/3}(v)},\quad v=\frac{2}{3}(-\hat{\sigma})^{3/2}.

(4.16)

Similarly to (
4.8
), we have

\psi_{a}=-\frac{4}{3}i(-\hat{\sigma})^{3/2}=-2iv.

(4.17)

Lemma 4.6

Asymptotic expansion (
4.9
) implies that

\hat{\chi}=\sqrt{-\hat{\sigma}}[1+O(|v|^{-1})]\ {\rm for}\ \operatorname{Im}\sigma=0,\ \sigma\leq 0,\ R=R_{+}=\exp(-(2\pi i)/3).

(4.18)

For
\tau\in\gamma_{1}
(respectively,
\tau\in\gamma_{2}
) the equilibrium
z_{1,a}(\tau)
is

\hat{\zeta}=-\sqrt{-\hat{s}}
(respectively,
\hat{\chi}=\sqrt{-\hat{\sigma}}
). One can establish this by considering stability of the linearised near
this equilibrium equation when time changes across
\gamma_{1}
(respectively, across
\gamma_{2}
).

Consider any solution to equation (
4.3
). On
\gamma_{1}
it has the form (
4.7
) with
R=R_{l}
. On

\gamma_{2}
the same solution has the form (
4.16
)
with
R=R_{r}
. The following relation between
R_{l}
and
R_{r}

follows from the previous transformation formulas and asymptotic
formulas for Bessel functions at the point
v=0
(
4.10
).

Lemma 4.7

R_{r}=R_{l}e^{-2\pi i/3}.

(4.19)

The system (
4.3
) has two special solutions which
have, respectively, asymptotic behaviour (
4.11
) on
\gamma_{1}
and
(
4.18
) on
\gamma_{2}
. These are different solutions because the relation
(
4.19
) is not satisfied for them. Splitting of these two solutions is the
reason for existence of maximal delay time.
The first of these special solutions, which is described by formula
(
4.7
) with
R=R_{l}=R_{-}=\exp((2\pi i)/3)
in variables
\zeta,s
, is described by formula (
4.16
)
with
R=R_{r}=R_{-}e^{-2\pi i/3}=1
in variables
\chi,\sigma
. This solution has poles on
\gamma_{2}

because the function
J_{-1/3}(v)+J_{1/3}(v)
has zeroes on
the positive real semi-axis
[
13
]
, Sec. 7.9. Inside the angle between
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
we have
{\rm Im\,}v<0
(here
v=\frac{2}{3}(-\hat{\sigma})^{3/2}
).

Lemma 4.8

For
{\rm Im\,}v<0
the function (
4.16
) with
R\neq e^{2\pi i/3}
has the expansion

\hat{\chi}=\sqrt{-\hat{\sigma}}\Bigl[1-2e^{-2iv}e^{\pi i/6}\frac{R-e^{-2\pi i/3}}{R-e^{2\pi i/3}}+O\Bigl(e^{-4|{\rm Im\,}v|}+\frac{1}{|v|}\Bigr)\Bigr].

(4.20)

Lemma 4.9

For the function
\hat{\zeta}
(
4.7
) we have

\frac{\partial\hat{\zeta}}{\partial R}=-i\frac{3\sqrt{3}}{2\pi\hat{s}}\frac{1}{(RJ_{-1/3}(v)+J_{1/3}(v))^{2}},\quad v=\frac{2}{3}(-\hat{s})^{3/2}.

(4.21)

For the derivative
{\partial\hat{\chi}}/{\partial R}
such relation is valid with the replacement of
\hat{s}
with
\hat{\sigma}
.

In the domain bounded by rays
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
, consider the solution of equation (
4.3
) that in variables
\hat{\chi},\hat{\sigma}
is described by the formula (
4.16
) with
R=1
. This solution has poles on
\gamma_{2}
(i.e. for real
\hat{\sigma}<0
) and does not have other singularities. This is because its poles are related to zeros of the Airy function
\rm{Ai(z)}
:

{\rm Ai}(z)=\sqrt{\frac{-z}{9}}\left[J_{-1/3}(\frac{2}{3}(-z)^{\frac{3}{2}})+J_{1/3}(\frac{2}{3}(-z)^{\frac{3}{2}})\right],

and all zeros of
\rm{Ai(z)}
are located on the negative real axis of the complex plane. In the variables
\hat{\zeta},\hat{s}
this solution is described by formula (
4.7
) with
R=\exp((2\pi i)/3)
. This implies
5
5

5

Indeed, if function
\hat{\zeta}
is regular at some
v
, but the denominator in (
4.7
) vanishes at this
v
, then the numerator in (
4.7
) should also vanish at this
v
. This would imply that
J_{1/3}(v)J_{2/3}(v)+J_{-1/3}(v)J_{-2/3}(v)
is equal to 0.
However, according to
[
13
]
, Sec. 7.11, formula (33), this expression is equal to
{\sqrt{3}}/({\pi v})
.

that, in the considered domain, the function
\exp((2\pi i)/3)J_{-1/3}(v)+J_{1/3}(v),v=\frac{2}{3}(-\hat{s})^{3/2}
has zeros only on the ray
\gamma_{2}
(which has equation

\hat{s}=\exp((2\pi i)/3)\hat{\sigma}
with real
\hat{\sigma}<0
).

4.3
Additional notation

Denote

L_{*}=\{x,\kappa\ :\ x=X(\kappa),\kappa=\mathrm{K}(\tau),\tau\in K_{*}\}.

Take
c_{l,1}^{-1}<\operatorname{Im}\tau_{c}
and denote

\displaystyle K_{*}^{+}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\tau\,:\,\tau\in K_{*},\,\operatorname{Im}\tau\geq-c_{l,1}^{-1}\},\ K_{*}^{-}=\{\tau\,:\,\tau\in K_{*},\,\operatorname{Im}\tau\leq c_{l,1}^{-1}\},

\displaystyle L_{*}^{+}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{x,\kappa\ :\ x=X(\kappa),\kappa=\mathrm{K}(\tau),\tau\in K_{*}^{+}\},\,L_{*}^{-}=\{x,\kappa\ :\ x=X(\kappa),\kappa=\mathrm{K}(\tau),\tau\in K_{*}^{-}\}.

Denote by
W
the
c_{l,2}^{-1}
-neighbourhood of
L_{*}
, where
c_{l,2}^{-1}
is such that right hand sides of the system (
1.1
) are analytic for
(x,\kappa)\in W
.
Denote by
W^{+}
and
W^{-}
the
c_{l,2}^{-1}
-neighbourhoods of
L_{*}^{+}
and
L_{*}^{-}
, respectively.
Denote by
V,V^{+},V^{-}
the projections of
W,W^{+},W^{-}
onto the
\kappa
-space.

Denote

\displaystyle K^{+}_{*,\delta}

\displaystyle=\{\tau\ :\ \tau\in K_{*}^{+},\ \operatorname{Im}\tau<\operatorname{Im}\tau_{c}-\delta\},\ K^{-}_{*,\delta}=\{\tau\ :\ \tau\in K_{*}^{-},\ \operatorname{Im}\tau>\operatorname{Im}\bar{\tau}_{c}+\delta\},

(4.22)

\displaystyle K_{*,\delta}

\displaystyle=K^{+}_{*,\delta}\cup K^{-}_{*,\delta}.

(Figure
5
)

Figure 5:
Slow time domain
K_{*,\delta}

Here
\delta=C_{\delta}\varepsilon^{2/3}
, where
C_{\delta}
is a sufficiently large constant whose value will be defined later, at the end of Section
5
.

Denote

B_{*,{\delta}}^{\pm}=\{\kappa\ :\ \kappa=\mathrm{K}(\tau),\tau\in K^{\pm}_{*,\delta}\},\ B_{*,{\delta}}=B_{*,{\delta}}^{+}\cup B_{*,{\delta}}^{-}\,.

(4.23)

Lemma 4.10

If
C_{\delta}>c_{l,3}
, then in a
c_{l,4}^{-1}\varepsilon^{2/3}
-neighbourhood of
B_{*,{\delta}}
(in the complex
\kappa
-space) the equilibrium
X(\kappa)
of the fast system is well defined and is an analytic function of
\kappa
.

The proof is a direct application of the Implicit Function Theorem with quantitative estimates. For
\kappa_{*}\in B_{*,{\delta}},\,x_{*}=X(\kappa_{*})
we have

|{\det}\left(\partial f(x_{*},\kappa_{*},0)/\partial x\right)|>k_{1}^{-1}|\kappa_{*}-\kappa_{c}|^{1/2}
. Hence, for
\kappa
satisfying

|\kappa-\kappa_{*}|<k_{2}^{-1}\varepsilon^{2/3}
, there exists an equilibrium
X(\kappa)
of the fast system such that

|X(\kappa)-x_{*}|<k_{3}|\kappa_{*}-\kappa_{c}|^{-1/2}|\kappa-\kappa_{*}|<k_{4}\varepsilon^{1/3}
, i.e.
(X(\kappa),\kappa)\in W
. We omit the detailed proof.

We denote the
c_{l,4}^{-1}\varepsilon^{2/3}
-neighbourhoods of
B_{*,{\delta}}^{\pm}
by
V_{{\delta}}^{\pm}
,
and denote
V_{{\delta}}=V_{{\delta}}^{+}\cup V_{{\delta}}^{-}
.

Thus, the slow equation (
1.2
) is well defined for
\kappa\in V_{{\delta}}
.

Lemma 4.11

The solution
\mathrm{K}
of the slow equation (
1.2
) can be continued to a

c_{l,5}^{-1}\varepsilon^{2/3}
-neighbourhoods of
the domains
K^{\pm}_{*,\delta}
.

The proof is a direct application of the Existence and Uniqueness Theorem with estimates for analytic ODEs. We omit the proof.

Denote by
\hat{K}^{\pm}_{*,\delta}
the parts of
c_{l,5}^{-1}\varepsilon^{2/3}
-neighbourhoods of
the domains
K^{\pm}_{*,\delta}
for which

\operatorname{Im}\bar{\tau}_{c}+\delta<\operatorname{Im}\tau<\operatorname{Im}\tau_{c}-\delta
. Denote
\hat{K}_{*,\delta}=\hat{K}^{+}_{*,\delta}\cup\hat{K}^{-}_{*,\delta}
.

Denote

\displaystyle W_{\delta}=\{x,\kappa\ :\ (x,\kappa)\in W,\ \kappa\in V_{{\delta}}\},\quad W_{\delta}^{\pm}=\{x,\kappa\ :\ (x,\kappa)\in W,\ \kappa\in V_{{\delta}}^{\pm}\}.

(4.24)

Denote
\tilde{d}_{+}=\tilde{d}_{+}(\kappa)=b\cdot(\kappa-\kappa_{c}),\ d_{+}=d_{+}(\kappa)=|\tilde{d}_{+}|,\ \tilde{d}_{-}=\tilde{d}_{-}(\kappa)=b\cdot(\kappa-\bar{\kappa}_{c}),\\
d_{-}=d_{-}(\kappa)=|\tilde{d}_{-}|
.

Lemma 4.12

If the constant
c_{l,4}
in Lemma
4.10
is chosen sufficiently large, then

c_{l,6}^{-1}d_{+}<|\kappa-\kappa_{c}|<c_{l,6}d_{+}
in
V_{\delta}^{+}
, and
c_{l,6}^{-1}d_{-}<|\kappa-\bar{\kappa}_{c}|<c_{l,6}d_{-}
in
V_{\delta}^{-}
.

In what follows we take such
c_{l,4}
. We choose
c_{l,5}
such that the solution of the slow system with
\tau
from the
c_{l,5}^{-1}\varepsilon^{2/3}
-neighbourhood of
the domain
K_{*,\delta}
does not leave
V_{{\delta}}
.

5
Transformations of variables

For
(x,\kappa)\in W_{\delta}
, introduce
\xi=x-X(\kappa)
as a new variable. In the statements and proofs of lemmas in this Section we use, in addition to the notation
O(\cdot)
, the notation
O^{*}(\cdot)
, which indicates that the function inside the
O
-symbol is a homogeneous polynomial in
\xi
of a specified degree. For example,
O^{*}(d_{+}^{-1/2})
and
O^{*}(|\xi|)
, are used, respectively, for terms that are independent of
\xi
and terms that are linear in
\xi
, etc.

Lemma 5.1

(a) For
C_{\delta}>c_{t,1}
,
in the variables
\xi,\kappa
the system (
1.1
) in the domain
W^{+}_{\delta}
takes the form

\displaystyle\dot{\xi}={\cal{A}}(\kappa)\xi+O(|\xi|^{2})+\varepsilon O^{*}(d_{+}^{-1/2}),\quad\dot{\kappa}=\varepsilon G(\kappa)+\varepsilon O(|\xi|)+O^{*}(\varepsilon^{2}),

(5.1)

\displaystyle{\cal{A}}=\partial f(X(\kappa),\kappa)/\partial x,\quad G=g(X(\kappa),\kappa,0).

Differentiation with respect to
\kappa
of
O
-terms that do not contain explicitly
d_{+}
multiplies order of these terms by
O(d_{+}^{-1/2})
. Differentiation with respect to
\kappa
of
O
-terms that contain explicitly
d_{+}
multiplies order of these terms by
O(d_{+}^{-1})
.
Derivative of

G
with respect to
\kappa
is
O(d_{+}^{-1/2})
.

(b) More accurate component-wise estimates are valid. For

(x,\kappa)\in W^{+}_{\delta}
, decompose
\xi
into projections onto the invariant subspace of the matrix
A_{c}
corresponding to the eigenvalue 0 (the first component of
\xi
) and onto the invariant subspace corresponding to all other eigenvalues. Then
O^{*}(d_{+}^{-1/2})
in the last term of the first equation in (
5.1
) can be replaced by
O^{*}(1)
for all components of
\dot{\xi}
except the first one.

In the domain
W^{-}_{\delta}
, analogous estimates hold, with
d_{+}
replaced by
d_{-}
.

Here and below estimates of
O
-terms are uniform in
C_{\delta}
unless stated otherwise.

Lemma 5.2

For
C_{\delta}>c_{t,2}
, one can make in system (
5.1
) a real-analytic transformation of variables

\xi=C(\kappa)\tilde{\xi}
such that the matrix
\tilde{\cal{A}}=C^{-1}{\cal{A}}C
has the block-diagonal form with blocks
\tilde{\cal{A}}_{0}
of size
2\times 2
and
\tilde{\cal{A}}_{1}
of size
(n-2)\times(n-2)
. The first block has the form

\tilde{\cal{A}}_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}&i(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})\\
i(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1})&\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}\end{pmatrix}.

In the new variables, system (
1.1
) in domain
W^{+}_{\delta}
takes the following form (we omit the ‘‘tildes’’ over the new variables)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\dot{\xi}=\left(\tilde{\cal{A}}(\kappa)+\varepsilon O^{*}(d_{+}^{-1/2})\right)\xi+O(|\xi|^{2})+\varepsilon O^{*}(d_{+}^{-1/2}),\quad

(5.2)

\displaystyle\dot{\kappa}=\varepsilon G(\kappa)+\varepsilon O(|\xi|)+O^{*}(\varepsilon^{2}).

Differentiation with respect to
\kappa
of
O
-terms that do not contain explicitly
d_{+}
multiplies these terms by
O(d_{+}^{-1/2})
. Differentiation with respect to
\kappa
of
O
-terms that contain explicitly
d_{+}
multiplies these terms by
O(d_{+}^{-1})
.

In the domain
W^{-}_{\delta}
, similar estimates hold with
d_{+}
replaced by
d_{-}
and with
z
and
w
interchanged.

Lemma 5.3

For
C_{\delta}>c_{t,3}
, in
W_{\delta}
there exists a real-analytic transformation of variables

\xi\mapsto\hat{\xi}

with the following properties.
In
W^{+}_{\delta}
this transformation
differs by
O(\varepsilon d_{+}^{-1})
from a linear (affine) transformation of
\xi
;
system (
1.1
) in the new variables takes the form (we omit the ‘‘hats’’ over the new variables)

\displaystyle\dot{\xi}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A(\kappa,\varepsilon)\xi+O(|\xi|^{2})+\varepsilon^{3}O^{*}(d_{+}^{-3}|\xi|)+\varepsilon^{3}O^{*}(d_{+}^{-7/2}),

(5.3)

\displaystyle\dot{\kappa}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon F(\kappa,\varepsilon)+\varepsilon O(|\xi|)+O^{*}(\varepsilon^{2}),

(5.4)

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g(X(\kappa),\kappa,0)+O^{*}(\varepsilon d_{+}^{-1}).

(5.5)

The matrix
A
is block-diagonal with blocks
A_{0}
of size

2\times 2
and
A_{1}
of size
(n-2)\times(n-2)
. These blocks are
O^{*}(\varepsilon d_{+}^{-1})
-close to
\tilde{\cal{A}}_{0}
and
\tilde{\cal{A}}_{1}
, respectively.
The first block has the form

A_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}\Lambda_{1}+\Lambda_{2}&i(\Lambda_{1}-\Lambda_{2})\\
i(\Lambda_{2}-\Lambda_{1})&\Lambda_{1}+\Lambda_{2}\end{pmatrix}.

If one uses variables
z=\xi_{1}+i\xi_{2},w=\xi_{1}-i\xi_{2}
instead of
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}
,
then for
(x,\kappa)\in W^{+}_{\delta}
the estimate
O^{*}(\varepsilon d_{+}^{-1})
for shift of variables can be replaced by
O^{*}(\varepsilon d_{+}^{-1/2})
for
w
and remaining components of
\xi
,
the term
O^{*}(d_{+}^{-7/2})
can be replaced by
O^{*}(d_{+}^{-3})
for
\dot{w}
and remaining components of
\dot{\xi}
.

Differentiation with respect to
\kappa
of
O
-terms that do not contain explicitly
d_{+}
multiplies these terms by
O(d_{+}^{-1/2})
. Differentiation with respect to
\kappa
of
O
-terms that contain explicitly
d_{+}
multiplies these terms by
O(d_{+}^{-1})
.

In the domain
W^{-}_{\delta}
, similar estimates hold with
d_{+}
replaced by
d_{-}
and with
z
and
w
interchanged.

Lemma 5.4

For system (
5.3
), for
C_{\delta}>c_{t,4}
and
|\hat{\xi}|<c_{t,5}\varepsilon^{1/3}
, in
W_{\delta}
one can make a real-analytic transformation of variables
(\xi,\kappa)=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\eta,\kappa)\mapsto(\hat{\xi},\kappa)=(\hat{\xi}_{1},\hat{\xi}_{2},\hat{\eta},\kappa)

that eliminates some quadratic, cubic, and fourth order terms in equations.
In the domain
W^{+}_{\delta}
this transformation in variables
z=\xi_{1}+i\xi_{2},w=\xi_{1}-i\xi_{2},\ \eta,\kappa
meets estimates

\displaystyle\hat{z}=z+O(|z|^{2}d_{+}^{-1/2}+|\xi|^{2}),\ \hat{w}=w+O(|zw|d_{+}^{-1/2}+|\xi|^{2}),\ \hat{\eta}=\eta+O(|\xi|^{2})

(‘‘hats’’ for new variables). The transformed system is such that the equation for
\dot{z}
contains terms
O(\varepsilon^{3}d_{+}^{-3}|\xi|)+O^{*}(\varepsilon^{3}d_{+}^{-7/2})
, and the remaining part of
\dot{z}
contains the monomial
z^{2}
with a coefficient
O^{*}(\varepsilon d_{+}^{-3/2})
, other quadratic monomials containing
z
and monomial
w^{2}
with coefficients
O^{*}(\varepsilon d_{+}^{-1/2})
, and all other quadratic monomials with coefficients
O^{*}(1)
(we omit the ‘‘hats’’ over the new variables). Higher order in
\xi
terms in equation for
\dot{z}
, that are not bounded above by estimates for quadratic terms, are estimated as

O(|\xi|_{*}^{3}d_{+}^{-1/2})+O(|z|^{5}d_{+}^{-3/2})
,
where ‘‘star’’ indicates that in computation of
|\xi|_{*}^{3}
the term
|z|^{3}
is not included (i.e.
6
6

6

Recall that
|\cdot|
is the sum of absolute values of coordinates of a vector.

|\xi|_{*}^{3}=|\xi|^{3}-|z|^{3}
assuming that
\xi
is represented in coordinates
z,w,\eta
).
The equation for
\dot{w}
contains terms
O(\varepsilon^{3}d_{+}^{-3}|\xi|)+O^{*}(\varepsilon^{3}d_{+}^{-3})
, and the remaining part of
\dot{w}
contains monomial
wz
with coefficient
O^{*}(\varepsilon d_{+}^{-3/2})
, monomials
z^{2},w^{2},w\eta
with coefficients
O^{*}(\varepsilon d_{+}^{-1/2})
, other quadratic monomials with coefficients
O^{*}(1)
.
The equation for
\dot{\eta}
contains terms
O(\varepsilon^{3}d_{+}^{-3}|\xi|)+O^{*}(\varepsilon^{3}d_{+}^{-3})
, and the remaining part of
\dot{\eta}
contains monomials
z^{2},w^{2}
with coefficients
O^{*}(\varepsilon d_{+}^{-1/2})
, other quadratic monomials with coefficients
O^{*}(1)
.
Higher order in
\xi
terms in equations for
\eta,w
are estimated, respectively, as
O(|\xi|_{*}^{3}d_{+}^{-1/2})+O(|z|^{4})
and
O(|\xi|_{*}^{3}d_{+}^{-1/2})+O(|z|^{3})
.

In the domain
W^{-}_{\delta}
, similar estimates hold with
d_{+}
replaced by
d_{-}
and with
z
and
w
interchanged.
The transformation of variables meets estimates

\displaystyle\hat{z}=z+O(|zw|d_{-}^{-1/2}+|\xi|^{2}),\ \hat{w}=w+O(|w|^{2}d_{-}^{-1/2}+|\xi|^{2}),\ \hat{\eta}=\eta+O(|\xi|^{2}).

In this domain, the equation for
\dot{z}
contains terms
O(\varepsilon^{3}d_{-}^{-3}|\xi|)+O^{*}(\varepsilon^{3}d_{-}^{-3})
, and in the remaining part of
\dot{z}
quadratic monomials are estimated as
O^{*}(|\eta|(|\eta|+|w|))+O^{*}(\varepsilon|zw|d_{-}^{-3/2})+O^{*}(\varepsilon|\xi|^{2}d_{-}^{-1/2})
, higher order terms, that are not bounded above by estimates for quadratic terms, are estimated as

O(|\xi|_{**}^{3}d_{-}^{-1/2})+O(|w|^{3}).

Here
|\xi|_{**}^{3}=|\xi|^{3}-|w|^{3}
.

Lemma 5.5

For
C_{\delta}>c_{t,6}
,
in
W_{\delta}
there exists a real-analytic transformation of variables
\xi,\kappa\mapsto\xi,\hat{\kappa}
with the following properties.
In
W^{+}_{\delta}
this transformation
satisfies estimates

\hat{\kappa}=\kappa+\varepsilon O(|z|d_{-}^{-1/2}+|\xi|_{*}).

Equation for
\hat{\kappa}
has the form (the ‘‘hats" are omitted)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\dot{\kappa}=\varepsilon G(\kappa)+O^{*}(\varepsilon^{2}d_{+}^{-1})+\varepsilon^{2}O(|z|^{2}d_{+}^{-2})+\varepsilon^{2}O(|\xi|_{*}^{2}d_{+}^{-3/2})

(5.6)

\displaystyle+\varepsilon O\left(|z|^{4}+|z|^{5}d_{+}^{-2}+|\xi|_{*}^{3}\right)+\varepsilon^{3}O(|z|d_{+}^{-3}+|\xi|_{*}d_{+}^{-3/2})+\varepsilon^{4}O(d_{+}^{-7/2}|\xi|).

In the domain
W^{-}_{\delta}
, similar estimates hold with
d_{+}
replaced by
d_{-}
and with
z
and
w
interchanged.

Let
\xi(t),\kappa(t)
be a solution of the system obtained from (
5.3
) after transformation from Lemma
5.4
. Consider the real initial conditions
\xi(t_{0}),\kappa(t_{0})
.

Lemma 5.6

Suppose that, in the complex
t
-plane, the solutions
\xi(t)
and
\kappa(t)
can be analytically continued to a neighbourhood
U
of a real interval. Then they can also be analytically continued to the reflected set
\overline{U}
, and

\xi(\bar{t})=\overline{\xi(t)},\qquad\kappa(\bar{t})=\overline{\kappa(t)},

for all
t\in U\cup\overline{U}
.

The proof is evident.

Introduce
z=\xi_{1}+i\xi_{2},w=\xi_{1}-i\xi_{2}
and rewrite the system in the variables
z,w,\eta=(\xi_{3},\ldots,\xi_{n}),\kappa
. Lemma
5.6
implies that
w(t)=\overline{z(\bar{t})}
for real-analytic solutions. This equation will be used instead of the differential equation for
w
. In the domain
W^{+}_{\delta}
we have

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\dot{z}=\Lambda_{1}(\kappa)z+\varepsilon O(|z|^{2}d_{+}^{-3/2})+\varepsilon O(|\xi|_{*}^{2}d_{+}^{-1/2})+O(|\eta|(|\eta|+|w|))

(5.7)

\displaystyle+O(|\xi|_{*}^{3}d_{+}^{-1/2})+O(|z|^{5}d_{+}^{-3/2})+\varepsilon^{3}O(d_{+}^{-3}|\xi|)+\varepsilon^{3}O^{*}(d_{+}^{-7/2}),

\displaystyle\dot{\eta}=B(\kappa)\eta+O(|\eta|^{2})+O(|\eta|(|z|+|w|))+O(|zw|)

\displaystyle+\varepsilon O((|z|^{2}+|w|^{2})d_{+}^{-1/2})+O(|\xi|_{*}^{3}d_{+}^{-1/2})+O(|z|^{4})+\varepsilon^{3}O(d_{+}^{-3}|\xi|)+\varepsilon^{3}O^{*}(d_{+}^{-3}),

\displaystyle\dot{\kappa}=\varepsilon F(\kappa)+\varepsilon^{2}O(|z|^{2}d_{+}^{-2})+\varepsilon^{2}O(|\xi|_{*}^{2}d_{+}^{-3/2})

\displaystyle+\varepsilon O\left(|z|^{4}+|z|^{5}d_{+}^{-2}+|\xi|_{*}^{3}\right)+\varepsilon^{3}O(|z|d_{+}^{-3}+|\xi|_{*}d_{+}^{-3/2})+\varepsilon^{4}O(d_{+}^{-7/2}|\xi|),

\displaystyle F=g(X(\kappa),\kappa,0)+\varepsilon O^{*}(d_{+}^{-1}).

Here the ‘‘star’’ in
|\xi|_{*}^{2},|\xi|_{*}^{3}
indicates that in the computation of that quantities the term
|z|^{2}
(respectively,
|z|^{3}
) is not included; that is,

|\xi|_{*}^{2}=|\xi|^{2}-|z|^{2},|\xi|_{*}^{3}=|\xi|^{3}-|z|^{3}

where
\xi
is represented in coordinates
z,w,\eta
.

In the domain
W^{-}_{\delta}
, similar estimates hold with
d_{+}
replaced by
d_{-}
,
\Lambda_{1}
replaced by

\Lambda_{2}
,
and with
z
and
w
interchanged.

Equation for
z
in
W^{-}_{\delta}
is

\displaystyle\dot{z}

\displaystyle=\Lambda_{1}(\kappa)z+O(|\eta|(|\eta|+|w|))+\varepsilon O(|z||w|d_{-}^{-3/2})+\varepsilon O(|\xi|^{2}d_{-}^{-1/2})+O(|\xi|_{**}^{3}d_{-}^{-1/2})+O(|w|^{3})

(5.8)

\displaystyle+\varepsilon^{3}O(d_{-}^{-3}|\xi|)+\varepsilon^{3}O^{*}(d_{-}^{-3}).

Here
|\xi|_{**}^{3}=|\xi|^{3}-|w|^{3}
.

The argument
\varepsilon
is omitted from the right hand sides of these equations for brevity. Instead of the system of
n+m
ordinary differential equations we obtain a system of
n+m-1
delay differential equations with an imaginary non-constant value of the delay.

Remark.
If condition H is dropped, then for
\kappa
near

\kappa_{c}
and
\bar{\kappa}_{c}
we would have the same equations (
5.7
),
(
5.8
). However, for
\kappa
far from

\kappa_{c}
and
\bar{\kappa}_{c}
, the term
O(|w|^{2})

should be added to the right hand side of the equation for
\dot{z}
.

From now on, we fix
C_{\delta}>c_{t,6}
.

6
Preliminary lemmas about continuation of solutions

6.1
Curves determined by improved slow equation

Consider ‘‘improved’’ slow equation (see (
5.7
))

\dot{\kappa}=\varepsilon F(\kappa),\ F=g(X(\kappa),\kappa,0)+O^{*}(\varepsilon d_{\pm}^{-1}),\ \kappa\in V_{\delta}^{\pm}.

(6.1)

Consider solution
\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\tau)
of equation (
6.1
) with some initial condition
\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{*}^{-})
of the form
\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{*}^{-})=\mathrm{K}(\tau_{*}^{-})+O(\varepsilon)
.
Without loss of generality, we assume that
\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\tau)
is well defined for
\tau\in K_{*,\delta}
(otherwise we would introduce a smaller time domain where both
\mathrm{K}
and
\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}
are well defined).
Denote
\hat{d}_{+}(\tau)=|b\cdot(\mathrm{K}(\tau)-\kappa_{c})|,\ \hat{d}_{-}=|b\cdot(\mathrm{K}(\tau)-\bar{\kappa}_{c})|,\\
\hat{d}_{+,\varepsilon}(\tau)=|b\cdot(\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\tau)-\kappa_{c})|,\ \hat{d}_{-,\varepsilon}=|b\cdot(\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\tau)-\bar{\kappa}_{c})|
.

Lemma 6.1

In the domain
K_{*,\delta}
we have

|\mathrm{K}(\tau)-\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\tau)|=O(\varepsilon(1+|\ln\hat{d}_{\pm}(\tau|)),\quad 0.5\hat{d}_{\pm}(\tau)<\hat{d}_{\pm,\varepsilon}(\tau)<2\hat{d}_{\pm}(\tau).

One should take
\hat{d}_{+}
for
\operatorname{Im}\tau>-c_{l,1}^{-1}
, and
\hat{d}_{-}
for
\operatorname{Im}\tau<c_{l,1}^{-1}
.

Introduce the ‘‘phase’’

\Psi_{\varepsilon}(\tau)=\int_{\tau_{*}}^{\tau}\Lambda_{1}(\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\vartheta))\,d\vartheta

(6.2)

and consider in the upper half-plane of the complex variable
\tau
arcs of level curves

\operatorname{Re}\Psi_{\varepsilon}={\rm const}
with endpoints on the real axis.

Take any point
\tau_{\gamma}
on
\gamma_{1}
close to
\tau_{c}
, but such that
\operatorname{Im}\tau_{\gamma}<\operatorname{Im}\tau_{c}-{\delta}
. According to Lemma
4.1
, the distance of
\tau_{\gamma}
from
\Gamma_{*,1}
is
O(|\tau_{\gamma}-\tau_{c}|^{3/2})
. Consider curves
\operatorname{Re}\Psi(\tau)={\rm const}
and
\operatorname{Re}\Psi_{\varepsilon}(\tau)={\rm const}
passing through
\tau_{\gamma}
(i.e.
\operatorname{Re}\Psi(\tau)=\operatorname{Re}\Psi(\tau_{\gamma})
and
\operatorname{Re}\Psi_{\varepsilon}(\tau)=\operatorname{Re}\Psi_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{\gamma}))
. The first of these curves crosses the real axis at a point
\tau_{-}
such that
|\tau_{-}-\tau_{*}^{-}|=O(|\tau_{\gamma}-\tau_{c}|^{2})
,
see Sect.
4.1
.

Lemma 6.2

The curve
\operatorname{Re}\Psi_{\varepsilon}(\tau)=\operatorname{Re}\Psi_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{\gamma})
crosses the real axis at a point
\tau_{*,\varepsilon,-}=\tau_{*,\varepsilon,-}(\tau_{\gamma})=\tau_{-}+O(\varepsilon\ln\varepsilon)
.

Thus,
\tau_{*,\varepsilon,-}=\tau_{-}+O(\varepsilon\ln\varepsilon)=\tau_{-}-\tau_{*}^{-}+\tau_{*}^{-}+O(\varepsilon\ln\varepsilon)=\tau_{*}^{-}+O(|\tau_{\gamma}-\tau_{c}|^{2})+O(\varepsilon\ln\varepsilon)
.
If
|\tau_{\gamma}-\tau_{c}|\sim\varepsilon^{2/3}
, then

\tau_{*,\varepsilon,-}=\tau_{*}^{-}+O(\varepsilon^{4/3})+O(\varepsilon\ln\varepsilon)=O(\varepsilon\ln\varepsilon)
.

The required value
\tau_{\gamma}
will be defined later. We will have

\tau_{*,\varepsilon,-}(\tau_{\gamma})-\tau_{*}^{-}=O(\varepsilon|\ln\varepsilon|)
. The estimates below are uniform with respect to
\tau_{*,\varepsilon,-}(\tau_{\gamma})
from the considered interval till the part of the paper where we choose
\tau_{\gamma}
. We will use a notation
\Gamma_{*,1,\varepsilon}
for the curve defined in Lemma
6.2
omitting an indication of dependence on
\tau_{\gamma}
.

Consider, in the
\tau
-plane, the line passing through the point
\tau_{\gamma}
and parallel to the real axis. Take any point
\tau_{p}
on this line with
\operatorname{Re}\tau_{\gamma}\leq\operatorname{Re}\tau_{p}\leq\operatorname{Re}\tau_{c}
. Consider curves
\operatorname{Re}\Psi(\tau)={\rm const}
and
\operatorname{Re}\Psi_{\varepsilon}(\tau)={\rm const}
passing through
\tau_{p}
(i.e.
\operatorname{Re}\Psi(\tau)=\operatorname{Re}\Psi(\tau_{p})
and
\operatorname{Re}\Psi_{\varepsilon}(\tau)=\operatorname{Re}\Psi_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{p}))
. The first of these curves is
\Gamma_{p}
from Section
4.1
. It crosses the real axis at a point
\tau_{p,-}=\tau_{-}+O(|\tau_{p}-\tau_{\gamma}|\sqrt{|\tau_{\gamma}-\tau_{c}|})=\tau_{-}+O(|\tau_{\gamma}-\tau_{c}|^{3/2})
. Denote the second of these curves as
\Gamma_{p,\varepsilon}
. Denote the point of intersection of this curve with the real axis by
\tau_{p,\varepsilon,-}
.

Lemma 6.3

The curve
\operatorname{Re}\Psi_{\varepsilon}(\tau)=\operatorname{Re}\Psi_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{p})
crosses the real axis at a point
\tau_{p,\varepsilon,-}=\tau_{-}+O(\varepsilon\ln\varepsilon)
.

Consider now a vertical line through
\tau_{c}
. Take a point
\tau_{q}
on this line below
\tau_{c}
, close to
\tau_{c}
, but such that
\operatorname{Im}\tau_{q}<\operatorname{Im}\tau_{c}-{\delta}
. Consider curves
\operatorname{Re}\Psi(\tau)={\rm const}
and
\operatorname{Re}\Psi_{\varepsilon}(\tau)={\rm const}
passing through
\tau_{q}
(i.e.
\operatorname{Re}\Psi(\tau)=\operatorname{Re}\Psi(\tau_{q})
and
\operatorname{Re}\Psi_{\varepsilon}(\tau)=\operatorname{Re}\Psi_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{q}))
. The first of these curves is
\Gamma_{q}
from Section
4.1
. It crosses the real axis at points
\tau_{q,\pm}=\tau_{*}^{\pm}+O(|\tau_{q}-\tau_{c}|^{3/2})
. Denote the second of these curves by

\Gamma_{q,\varepsilon}
. Denote points of intersection of this curve with the real axis by
\tau_{q,\varepsilon,\pm}
.

Lemma 6.4

The curve
\operatorname{Re}\Psi_{\varepsilon}(\tau)=\operatorname{Re}\Psi_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{q})
crosses the real axis at points
\tau_{q,\varepsilon,\pm}=\tau_{q,\pm}+O(\varepsilon\ln\varepsilon)
.

Thus,
\tau_{q,\varepsilon,\pm}=\tau_{*}^{\pm}+O(|\tau_{q}-\tau_{c}|^{3/2})+O(\varepsilon\ln\varepsilon)
. In particular, if

|\tau_{q}-\tau_{c}|\sim\varepsilon^{2/3}
, then
\tau_{q,\varepsilon,\pm}=\tau_{*}^{\pm}+O(\varepsilon\ln\varepsilon)
.

We can introduce now domains
D_{\gamma}
,
D_{p}
and
D_{q}
in
\tau
-plane to which solutions will be continued.

Domain
D_{\gamma}
is bounded by the curve
\Gamma_{*,1,\varepsilon}
, by the complex conjugate to it curve

\bar{\Gamma}_{*,1,\varepsilon}
, and by the lines
\operatorname{Im}\tau=\operatorname{Im}\tau_{\gamma}
,
\operatorname{Im}\tau=-\operatorname{Im}\tau_{\gamma}
,

\operatorname{Re}\tau=\operatorname{Re}\tau_{\gamma}
.

Domain
D_{p}
is bounded by the curve
\Gamma_{p,\varepsilon}
, by the complex conjugate to it curve
\bar{\Gamma}_{p,\varepsilon}
, and by the lines
\operatorname{Im}\tau=\operatorname{Im}\tau_{\gamma}
,
\operatorname{Im}\tau=-\operatorname{Im}\tau_{\gamma},\operatorname{Re}\tau=\operatorname{Re}\tau_{p}
.

Domain
D_{up}
is the union of domains
D_{p}
for all the considered values
\tau_{p}
.

Domain
D_{q}
is bounded by the curve
\Gamma_{q,\varepsilon}
and by the complex conjugate to it curve

\bar{\Gamma}_{q,\varepsilon}
.

6.2
Continuation into domains
D_{\gamma},D_{up}
.

Denote
t_{3}=\tau_{*,\varepsilon,-}(\tau_{\gamma})/\varepsilon
. Let
\operatorname{Im}\tau_{\gamma}=\operatorname{Im}\tau_{c}-(C_{\gamma}+o(1))\varepsilon^{2/3}
, where
C_{\gamma}
is a constant,
C_{\gamma}>C_{\delta}
. Consider solution
z(t),\eta(t),\kappa(t)
of system (
5.7
) with initial conditions of the form

|\kappa(t_{3})-\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon t_{3})|=O(\varepsilon^{6}|\ln\varepsilon|),\ |z(t_{3})|=O(\varepsilon^{3}),\ |\eta(t_{3})|=O(\varepsilon^{3}).

(6.3)

Lemma 6.5

If
C_{\gamma}>c_{e,1}
, then
the solution
z(t),\eta(t),\kappa(t)
can be analytically continued into the domains
D_{\gamma}
and
D_{up}
with the following estimates.

If
\operatorname{Im}\tau>-c_{l,1}^{-1}
, then

|\kappa(t)-\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon t)|<c_{e,2}\varepsilon^{4}\hat{d}_{+}^{-9/2},\ |z(t)|<c_{e,3}\varepsilon^{2}\hat{d}_{+}^{-5/2},\ |\eta(t)|<c_{e,4}\varepsilon^{3}\hat{d}_{+}^{-3}.

(6.4)

If
-2c_{l,1}^{-1}<\operatorname{Im}\tau<2c_{l,1}^{-1}
, then

|\kappa(t)-\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon t)|=O(\varepsilon^{4}),\ |z(t)|=O(\varepsilon^{3}),\ |\eta(t)|=O(\varepsilon^{3}).

(6.5)

If
\operatorname{Im}\tau<c_{l,1}^{-1}
, then

|\kappa(t)-\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon t)|<c_{e,2}\varepsilon^{4}\hat{d}_{-}^{-9/2},\ |z(t)|<c_{e,4}\varepsilon^{2}\hat{d}_{-}^{-3},\ |\eta(t)|<c_{e,4}\varepsilon^{3}\hat{d}_{-}^{-3}.

(6.6)

Constants in this Lemma depend on constants in estimates ‘‘
O(\cdot)
’’ for initial conditions in (
6.3

Lemmas on the continuation of solutions into the domains
D_{\gamma},D_{up},D_{q,l},andD_{q,r}
will be used in the proof of Theorem
1
as follows. We will show that the considered solution can be continued into

and

with

for any sufficiently large constant

. We will also show that this solution can be continued into

with

, provided that

is sufficiently large. Continuation into

and
D_{up}
will be used in continuation into a larger domain
