with vertices at
\tau_{c}
and
\bar{\tau}_{c}
. Continuation into
D_{q,l}
will be used to extend the solution into
D_{q,r}
, provided
C_{q}
is sufficiently large.

7
Proof of Theorem 1.

7.1
Initial part of motion

Let
x(t),\kappa(t)
be the solution of system (
1.1
) with

\kappa(t_{0})=\kappa_{0}=\mathrm{K}(\tau_{0})
,
t_{0}=\tau_{0}/\varepsilon
. According to
[
31
]
, p.55, if the initial point is in a
C_{2}^{-1}
-neighbourhood of the equilibrium of the fast system, then for some
t_{1}=t_{0}+O(|\ln\varepsilon|)
the solution comes to an
O(\varepsilon)
-neighbourhood of the equilibrium, and
\kappa(t_{1})=\mathrm{K}(\varepsilon t_{1})+O(\varepsilon)
.
After this, up to a slow time
\tau_{*}-c_{m,1}^{-1}
the phase point moves in an
O(\varepsilon)
-neighbourhood of the equilibrium, and
\kappa(t)=\mathrm{K}(\varepsilon t)+O(\varepsilon)
. Moreover, for description of this motion one can use the same variables as in Lemmas
5.3
,
5.4
. In these variables, starting from a slow time
\varepsilon t_{1}+c_{m,2}\varepsilon|\ln\varepsilon|
, we have

\xi(t)=O(\varepsilon^{3}),\kappa(t)=\mathrm{K}(\varepsilon t)+O(\varepsilon)
. Thus, we can take
\varepsilon t_{2}=\tau_{*}^{-}+O(\varepsilon|\ln\varepsilon|)

such that at
t=t_{2}
the solution in these new variables is in an
O(\varepsilon^{3})
-neighbourhood of the equilibrium
z=0,\eta=0
, and
\kappa(t_{2})=\mathrm{K}(\varepsilon t_{2})+O(\varepsilon)
(we assume that the constant
C_{1}
in the statement of Theorem
1
is chosen such that for
\tau_{0}<\tau_{*}^{-}-C_{1}\varepsilon|\ln\varepsilon|
we have
\varepsilon t_{2}<\tau_{*}^{-}
).

7.2
First part of the principal part of motion

We consider solution
z(t),\eta(t),\kappa(t)
of system (
5.7
). Take as the initial condition for the ‘‘improved’’ slow equation at
t=\tau_{*}^{-}/\varepsilon
the value

\kappa(\tau_{*}^{-}/\varepsilon)
:
\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{*}^{-})=\kappa(\tau_{*}^{-}/\varepsilon)
. Then the assumption
\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{*}^{-})=\mathrm{K}(\tau_{*}^{-})+O(\varepsilon)
from Section
6.1
is satisfied.
Take value
\tau_{\gamma}
(see Section
6.1
) such that
\hat{d}_{+}(\tau_{\gamma})=C_{\gamma,*}\varepsilon^{2/3}
with some not yet defined constant
C_{\gamma,*}
. Then
\operatorname{Im}\tau_{\gamma}=\operatorname{Im}\tau_{c}-(C_{\gamma}+o(1))\varepsilon^{2/3}
, where
C_{\gamma}
is a constant determined by the constant
C_{\gamma,*}
. Denote

t_{3}=\tau_{-,*,\varepsilon}(\tau_{\gamma})/\varepsilon
. We will choose the constant
C_{1}
in formulation of
Theorem
1
such that
\varepsilon t_{3}>\tau_{*}^{-}-C_{1}\varepsilon\ln|\varepsilon|
.

Take value
\tau_{q}
(see Section
6.1
) such that
\operatorname{Im}\tau_{q}=\operatorname{Im}\tau_{c}-C_{q}\varepsilon^{2/3}
, with some not yet defined constant
C_{q}
. Denote
t_{4}=\tau_{q,\varepsilon,-}/\varepsilon
.

We assume that
C_{\gamma}>C_{\delta}
,
C_{q}>C_{\delta}
.

Lemma 7.1

|\kappa(t_{3,4})-\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon t_{3,4})|<c_{m,3}\varepsilon^{6}|\ln\varepsilon|,\ |z(t_{3,4})|<c_{m,4}\varepsilon^{3},\ |\eta(t_{3,4})|<c_{m,5}\varepsilon^{3}.

(7.1)

(Recall that
\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{*}^{-})=\kappa(\tau_{*}^{-}/\varepsilon)
and
\varepsilon t_{3,4}=\tau_{-,*}+O(\varepsilon\ln\varepsilon)
).

According to Lemmas
6.5
,
6.6
, and
6.8
, the considered solution can be analytically continued into domains
D_{\gamma},D_{up}
, and
D_{q}
for sufficiently large
C_{\gamma,*}
and
C_{q}
with estimates given by these lemmas. These estimates are stated in terms of the variables obtained by transformations in Lemmas
5.3
,
5.4
, and
5.5
. For the original variables
z,w,\eta,\kappa
, the same lemmas imply the following.

In
D_{\gamma},D_{up}
and
D_{q,l}
for
\operatorname{Im}\tau>-c_{l,1}^{-1}
we have

|\kappa(t)-\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon t)|<c_{m,6}\varepsilon^{3}\hat{d}_{+}^{-3},\ |z(t)|<c_{m,7}\varepsilon\hat{d}_{+}^{-1},\ |\eta(t)|<c_{m,8}\varepsilon\hat{d}_{+}^{-1/2}.

(7.2)

In
D_{\gamma},D_{up}
and
D_{q,l}
for
\operatorname{Im}\tau<c_{l,1}^{-1}
we have

|\kappa(t)-\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon t)|<c_{m,6}\varepsilon^{3}\hat{d}_{-}^{-3},\ |z(t)|<c_{m,9}\varepsilon\hat{d}_{-}^{-1/2},\ |\eta(t)|<c_{m,8}\varepsilon\hat{d}_{-}^{-1/2}.

(7.3)

Values
z(t),\eta(t)
are obtained from the components of the vector

C^{-1}(\kappa(t))(x(t)-X(\kappa(t)))
. Denote by
\hat{z}(t),\hat{\eta}(t)
the corresponding quantities obtained in the same way from the components of the vector

C^{-1}(\mathrm{K}(\varepsilon t))(x(t)-X(\mathrm{K}(\varepsilon t)))
.

Lemma 7.2

For
\operatorname{Im}\tau>-c_{l,1}^{-1}
, we have

\displaystyle|\hat{z}(t)|<c_{m,10}\varepsilon\hat{d}_{+}^{-1},|\hat{\eta}(t)|<c_{m,11}\varepsilon\hat{d}_{+}^{-1/2}.

(7.4)

For
\operatorname{Im}\tau<c_{l,1}^{-1}
, we have

\displaystyle|\hat{z}(t)|<c_{m,12}\varepsilon\hat{d}_{-}^{-1/2},\ |\hat{\eta}(t)|<c_{m,11}\varepsilon\hat{d}_{-}^{-1/2}.

(7.5)

Now we should obtain estimate that would later specify the value
\tau_{\gamma}
, which also specifies the curve
\Gamma_{*,1,\varepsilon}
. We have chosen the point
\tau_{\gamma}
on the ray
\gamma_{1}
such that
\operatorname{Im}\tau_{\gamma}<\operatorname{Im}\tau_{c}-C_{\delta}\varepsilon^{2/3}
and
\hat{d}_{+}(\tau_{\gamma})=C_{\gamma,*}\varepsilon^{2/3}
, where
C_{\gamma,*}
is a sufficiently large positive constant, which we will specify later in Section
7.4
. Recall that the curve
\Gamma_{*,1,\varepsilon}
and the ray
\gamma_{1}
intersect at

\tau=\tau_{\gamma}
. Thus, estimates (
7.2
) are satisfied at this value of
\tau
. We should specify the value
C_{\gamma,*}
. For
t_{\gamma}=\tau_{\gamma}/\varepsilon
, we have
|\hat{z}(t_{\gamma})|<c_{m,10}\varepsilon\hat{d}_{+}^{-1}=\frac{c_{m,10}}{C_{\gamma,*}}\varepsilon^{1/3},\ |\hat{\eta}(t_{\gamma})|<c_{m,11}\varepsilon\hat{d}_{+}^{-1/2}=\frac{c_{m,11}}{C_{\gamma,*}^{1/2}}\varepsilon^{2/3}
. Values
\hat{z}(t_{\gamma}),\hat{\eta}(t_{\gamma})
are the projections of the deviations of the phase point from
X(\mathrm{K}(\tau_{\gamma}))
onto the eigenspaces of the equilibrium
X(\mathrm{K}(\tau_{\gamma}))
of the fast system. These estimates guarantee that the exact solution is close to the equilibrium of the fast system at
\tau=\tau_{\gamma}
, if
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small. We should choose
C_{\gamma,*}
sufficiently large to guarantee that the exact solution is close to the special solution with
R=R_{-}=\exp(2\pi i/3)
of Riccati equation
(
4.3
). For this special solution, at
\tau=\tau_{\gamma}
, we have (see (
4.11
))

|\hat{\zeta}-(-\sqrt{-\hat{s}})|<c_{m,13}|\sqrt{-\hat{s}}\,v^{-1}|,

where
\hat{\zeta},\hat{s},v
are the variables introduced in Section
4.2
. In the original variables this is reduced to

|z_{1}-\sqrt{-a^{-1}bg_{c}(\tau-\tau_{c})}|<\frac{c_{m,14}}{C_{\gamma,*}}\varepsilon^{1/3}.

Thus, at
\tau=\tau_{\gamma}
:

- the distance of the actual solution from the equilibrium
X((\kappa(\tau_{\gamma}/\varepsilon))
of the fast system is

O(\varepsilon\hat{d}_{+}^{-1})=O(\varepsilon^{1/3}/C_{\gamma,*})
in
z
, and
O(\varepsilon\hat{d}_{+}^{-1/2})=O(\varepsilon^{2/3}/C_{\gamma,*}^{1/2})
in
\eta,w
;

- the value
\mathrm{K}(\tau_{\gamma})
differs from the value of
\kappa
in the simplified (expanded) slow system by

O(|\tau_{\gamma}-\tau_{c}|^{3/2})=O((C_{\gamma,*}\varepsilon^{2/3})^{3/2})=O(C_{\gamma,*}^{3/2}\varepsilon);

-the value
\kappa(t)
differs from the value of
\kappa
in the simplified (expanded) slow system by
(this is the sum of difference of
\kappa
and
\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}
, difference of
\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}
and
\mathrm{K}
, difference of
\mathrm{K}
and the value of
\kappa
in the simplified (expanded) slow system)

O(c_{m,6}\varepsilon^{3}\hat{d}_{+}^{-3}+\varepsilon(1+|\ln\hat{d}_{+}(\tau|)+(C_{\gamma,*}^{3/2}\varepsilon))=O(\varepsilon|\ln\varepsilon|+C_{\gamma,*}^{3/2}\varepsilon);

- the distance of the equilibrium
X(\mathrm{K}(\tau_{\gamma}))
from the equilibrium of the simplified (expanded) fast system is
O(C_{\gamma,*}\varepsilon^{2/3})
in
z_{1}
(here
z_{1}
is the first coordinate in the eigen-coordinate system for
\kappa=\kappa_{c}
) and
O(C_{\gamma,*}^{3/2}\varepsilon)
in
\eta
.
(The second of these values is obtained as a difference between
\mathrm{K}(\tau_{\gamma})
and the value of
\kappa
in the simplified (expanded) slow system, which is
O(|\tau_{\gamma}-\tau_{c}|^{3/2})=O(C_{\gamma,*}^{3/2}\varepsilon)
. The first of these values is obtained as the product of this difference and
\hat{d}_{+}^{-1/2}
, which is
O(|\tau_{\gamma}-\tau_{c}|^{3/2}|\tau_{\gamma}-\tau_{c}|^{-1/2})=O(|\tau_{\gamma}-\tau_{c}|)=C_{\gamma,*}\varepsilon^{2/3}
. These estimates also take into account a difference of eigen-axes of equilibria for
\kappa=\mathrm{K}(\tau_{\gamma})
and
\kappa=\kappa_{c}
.)

Thus, at
\tau=\tau_{\gamma}
, in the variable
z_{1}
, the distance between the actual solution and the special solution can be calculated as

the distance between the actual solution and the equilibrium of the fast system at
\kappa=\kappa(t_{\gamma})
,

O(c_{m,10}\varepsilon\hat{d}_{+}^{-1}),

+

the distance between the equilibrium of the fast system at
\kappa=\kappa(t_{\gamma})
and the equilibrium of the fast system at
\kappa=\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{\gamma})
,

O(\hat{d}_{+}^{-1/2}c_{m,6}\varepsilon^{3}\hat{d}_{+}^{-3}),

+

the distance between the equilibrium of the fast system at
\kappa=\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{\gamma})
and the equilibrium of the fast system at
\kappa=\mathrm{K}(\tau_{\gamma})
),

O(\hat{d}_{+}^{-1/2}\varepsilon(1+|\ln\hat{d}_{+}(\tau|)),

+

the distance between the equilibrium of the fast system at
\kappa=\mathrm{K}(\tau_{\gamma})
and the equilibrium of the expanded system at
\tau=\tau_{\gamma}
),

O((|\tau_{\gamma}-\tau_{c}|^{-1/2}(|\tau_{\gamma}-\tau_{c}|^{3/2})=O(|\tau_{\gamma}-\tau_{c}|),

+

the distance between the equilibrium of the expanded system at
\tau=\tau_{\gamma}
and the special solution at

\tau=\tau_{\gamma}
),

O(\frac{c_{m,14}}{C_{\gamma,*}}\varepsilon^{1/3})
.

Thus, we get the following estimate for this value

\displaystyle O(c_{m,10}\varepsilon\hat{d}_{+}^{-1})+O(\hat{d}_{+}^{-1/2}c_{m,6}\varepsilon^{3}\hat{d}_{+}^{-3})+O(\hat{d}_{+}^{-1/2}\varepsilon(1+|\ln\hat{d}_{+}(\tau|))+O(|\tau_{\gamma}-\tau_{c}|)+O(\frac{c_{m,14}}{C_{\gamma,*}}\varepsilon^{1/3})

\displaystyle=O(\varepsilon^{1/3}/C_{\gamma,*}+\varepsilon^{2/3}/C_{\gamma,*}^{7/2}+\varepsilon^{2/3}|\ln\varepsilon|/C_{\gamma,*}^{1/2}+C_{\gamma,*}\varepsilon^{2/3})+O(\varepsilon^{1/3}/C_{\gamma,*})

\displaystyle=O(\varepsilon^{1/3}/C_{\gamma,*}+C_{\gamma,*}\varepsilon^{2/3}).

Denote by
z_{1,\varepsilon}(t)
the first component of the solution
x(t)
in the coordinate system constructed for the point
\kappa_{c}
. Denote by
z_{1,sp}(t)
the value
z_{1}
along the special solution. We have shown that

\displaystyle z_{1,\varepsilon}(t_{\gamma})-z_{1,sp}(t_{\gamma})=O(\varepsilon^{1/3}/C_{\gamma,*}+C_{\gamma,*}\varepsilon^{2/3}).

Introduce
R_{\gamma}
as the value of
R
in solution (
4.7
) that would give
z_{1}=z_{1,\varepsilon}(t_{\gamma})
at
t=t_{\gamma}
. The value of
R
that gives
z_{1,sp}(t)
is

R_{-}=\exp(2\pi i/3)
.

Lemma 7.3

\displaystyle R_{\gamma}-R_{-}=O(|z_{1,\varepsilon}(t_{\gamma})-z_{1,sp}(t_{\gamma})|/(C_{\gamma,*}^{1/2}\varepsilon^{1/3})).

Thus,

R_{\gamma}-R_{-}=O(1/C_{\gamma,*}^{3/2}+C_{\gamma,*}^{1/2}\varepsilon^{1/3}).

Denote

\tau_{\varepsilon}(t)=\tau_{c}+b\cdot(\kappa(t)-\kappa_{c})/(b\cdot g_{c}).

Here

g_{c}=g(x_{c},\kappa_{c},0)
.
Denote by
s_{\varepsilon}
the value obtained from
\tau_{\varepsilon}(t)
by the formulas expressing
s
via
\tau
(see Section
4.2
). Denote
\zeta_{\varepsilon}(t)=e^{-i\alpha_{1}}z_{1,\varepsilon}(t)
, and introduce corresponding values
\hat{s}_{\varepsilon}(t),\hat{\zeta}_{\varepsilon}(t)
, as in Section
4.2
.
Introduce
v_{\varepsilon}(t)=\frac{2}{3}(-\hat{s}_{\varepsilon}(t))^{3/2}
. We have

\displaystyle\tau_{\varepsilon}(t_{\gamma})

\displaystyle-\tau_{\gamma}=\tau_{c}+b\cdot(\kappa(t_{\gamma})-\kappa_{c})/(b\cdot g_{c})-\tau_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\tau_{c}+b\cdot(\mathrm{K}(\tau_{\gamma})-\kappa_{c})/(b\cdot g_{c})-\tau_{\gamma}+O(\kappa(t_{\gamma})-\mathrm{K}(\tau_{\gamma}))

\displaystyle=\tau_{c}+b\cdot(\kappa_{c}+g_{c}(\tau_{\gamma}-\tau_{c})+O(\tau_{\gamma}-\tau_{c})^{3/2}-\kappa_{c})/(b\cdot g_{c})-\tau_{\gamma}+O(\varepsilon|\ln\varepsilon|+C_{\gamma,*}^{-3}\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=O((\tau_{\gamma}-\tau_{c})^{3/2})+O(\varepsilon|\ln\varepsilon|+C_{\gamma,*}^{-3}\varepsilon)=O(\varepsilon|\ln\varepsilon|+C_{\gamma,*}^{3/2}\varepsilon).

Introduce
R_{\varepsilon}(t)
such that

\hat{\zeta}_{\varepsilon}(t)=-i\sqrt{-\hat{s}_{\varepsilon}(t)}\,\frac{J_{-2/3}(v_{\varepsilon}(t))-R_{\varepsilon}(t)J_{2/3}(v_{\varepsilon}(t))}{R_{\varepsilon}(t)J_{-1/3}(v_{\varepsilon}(t))+J_{1/3}(v_{\varepsilon}(t))}.

(7.6)

This value is well defined at least at
t=t_{\gamma}
.

Lemma 7.4

R_{\varepsilon}(t_{\gamma})-R_{\gamma}=O(|\tau_{\varepsilon}(t_{\gamma})-\tau_{\gamma}|/(C_{\gamma,*}\varepsilon^{2/3})).

Thus,

R_{\varepsilon}(t_{\gamma})-R_{\gamma}=O(\varepsilon^{1/3}|\ln\varepsilon|/C_{\gamma,*}+\varepsilon^{1/3}C_{\gamma,*}^{1/2}).

Therefore,

\displaystyle|R_{\varepsilon}(t_{\gamma})-R_{-}|\leq|R_{\varepsilon}(t_{\gamma})-R_{\gamma}|+|R_{\gamma}-R_{-}|

\displaystyle=O(\varepsilon^{1/3}|\ln\varepsilon|/C_{\gamma,*}+\varepsilon^{1/3}C_{*}^{1/2})+O(1/C_{\gamma,*}^{3/2}+\varepsilon^{1/3}C_{\gamma,*}^{1/2})

\displaystyle=O(1/C_{\gamma,*}^{3/2}+\varepsilon^{1/3}(C_{\gamma,*}^{1/2}+C_{\gamma,*}^{-1}|\ln\varepsilon|)).

For
C_{\gamma,*}>c_{m,15}
, we have

|R_{\varepsilon}(t_{\gamma})-R_{-}|<\frac{c_{m,16}}{C_{\gamma,*}^{3/2}}<\frac{1}{200}.

(7.7)

In what follows, we take
C_{\gamma,*}>c_{m,15}
.

7.3
Second part of the principal part of motion: continuation of solution up to singularity

Denote by
D_{\triangle}
the triangle in the complex slow time plane bounded by the lines
\gamma_{1},\operatorname{Im}\varepsilon t=\operatorname{Im}\tau_{\gamma},\operatorname{Re}\varepsilon t=\operatorname{Re}\tau_{c}
(Figure
7
). Let
\bar{D}_{\triangle}
be the triangle complex conjugate to
D_{\triangle}
. Denote

D_{c}=D_{up}\cup D_{\triangle}\cup\bar{D}_{\triangle}
(notation
D_{up}
is introduced at the end of Section
6.1
).
Denote by
z_{sm},w_{sm},\eta_{sm},\xi_{sm}
values analogous to
z,w,\eta,\xi
in Lemma
5.2
, but constructed for

x-X(\kappa_{c})
and matrix
A_{c}={\cal A}(\kappa_{c})
. Denote by
z_{ms},w_{ms},\eta_{ms},\xi_{ms}
values analogous to
z,w,\eta,\xi
in Lemma
5.2
, but constructed for

x-X(\bar{\kappa}_{c})
and matrix
{\cal A}(\bar{\kappa}_{c})
.

Figure 7:
Triangle
D_{\triangle}

Lemma 7.5

For
C_{\gamma,*}>c_{m,17}
, solution
x(t),\kappa(t)
can be analytically continued into the domain
D_{c}
with the following estimates.

If
\tau\in D_{\triangle}
, then

\displaystyle|\kappa(t)-\kappa_{c}|=O(\varepsilon^{2/3}),\ |z_{sm}(t)|=O(\varepsilon^{1/3}),\ |w_{sm}(t)|=O(\varepsilon^{2/3}),\ |\eta_{sm}(t)|=O(\varepsilon^{2/3}),

(7.8)

\displaystyle\kappa(t)=\overline{\kappa(\bar{t})},\ z_{sm}(t)=\overline{w_{ms}(\bar{t})},\ w_{sm}(t)=\overline{z_{ms}(\bar{t})},\ \eta_{sm}(t)=\overline{\eta_{ms}(\bar{t})}.

In this domain values

R_{\varepsilon}(t),\hat{s}_{\varepsilon}(t),\hat{\zeta}_{\varepsilon}(t)
are well defined and

\displaystyle

\displaystyle|R_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{c}/\varepsilon)-R_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{\gamma}/\varepsilon)|=O(\varepsilon^{1/3}),

(7.9)

\displaystyle|\hat{s}_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{c}/\varepsilon)|=O(\varepsilon^{1/3}\ln\varepsilon),\ |\kappa(\tau/\varepsilon)-\mathrm{K}(\tau)|=O(\varepsilon\ln\varepsilon),\ |\kappa(\tau_{c}/\varepsilon)-\kappa_{c}|=O(\varepsilon\ln\varepsilon),

(7.10)

\displaystyle|\hat{\zeta}_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{c}/\varepsilon)-\frac{1}{R_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{\gamma}/\varepsilon)}\frac{-2\pi i}{\Gamma^{2}(1/3)3^{1/6}}|=O(\varepsilon^{1/3}\ln\varepsilon).

The estimates for
\tau\in D_{up}

are given by Lemma
6.5
.

If
\tau\in\bar{D}_{\triangle}
, then

\displaystyle|\kappa(t)-\bar{\kappa}_{c}|=O(\varepsilon^{2/3}),\ |z_{ms}(t)|=O(\varepsilon^{2/3}),\ |w_{ms}(t)|=O(\varepsilon^{1/3}),\ |\eta_{ms}(t)|=O(\varepsilon^{2/3}).

(7.11)

Note that coefficients in
O(\cdot)
in this Lemma depend on the choice of the constant
C_{\gamma,*}
.

Note that
\kappa
in the text of this Lemma denotes the original variable
\kappa
, while in the text of Lemma
6.5
it denotes the variable transformed as described in Lemma
5.5
.

Corollary 7.1

Estimates (
7.7
) and (
7.9
) imply that

|R_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{c}/\varepsilon)-R_{-}|<\frac{2c_{m,16}}{C_{\gamma,*}^{3/2}}<\frac{1}{100}.

7.4
A domain to which the solution will be continued

In what follows, we take
C_{q}=C_{\gamma}
. Thus, we assume that
C_{\gamma}>{\rm max}\{c_{e,1},c_{e,10}\}
.

Recall that
R_{-}=e^{2\pi i/3}
. Then
R_{+}=e^{-2\pi i/3}R_{-}=1
.
Denote

R^{+}_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{c}/\varepsilon)=e^{-2\pi i/3}R_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{c}/\varepsilon).

Then

|R^{+}_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{c}/\varepsilon)-1|<\frac{2c_{m,16}}{C_{\gamma,*}^{3/2}}<\frac{1}{100}.

We will use functions
\hat{\sigma}_{\varepsilon}(t)=e^{-2\pi i/3}\hat{s}_{\varepsilon}(t)
and
\hat{\chi}_{\varepsilon}(t)=e^{2\pi i/3}\hat{\zeta}_{\varepsilon}(t)
. Then

\hat{\chi}_{\varepsilon}(t)=-i\sqrt{-\hat{\sigma}_{\varepsilon}(t)}\,\frac{J_{-2/3}(v_{\varepsilon}(t))-R^{+}_{\varepsilon}(t)J_{2/3}(v_{\varepsilon}(t))}{R^{+}_{\varepsilon}(t)J_{-1/3}(v_{\varepsilon}(t))+J_{1/3}(v_{\varepsilon}(t))},\ v_{\varepsilon}(t)=\frac{2}{3}(-\hat{\sigma}_{\varepsilon}(t))^{3/2}.

(7.12)

Indeed, we know that substitution
s=e^{2\pi i/3}\sigma
with the replacement of
R
with
e^{-2\pi i/3}R
in (
4.7
) results in multiplication of the right hand side of (
4.7
) by
e^{2\pi i/3}
(cf. (
4.7
) , (
4.16
) and
(
4.19
)). This implies (
7.12
). Left- and righ-thand sides of (
7.12
) are well defined at least for
\operatorname{Re}\tau=\operatorname{Re}\tau_{c},\operatorname{Im}\tau\leq\operatorname{Im}\tau_{c}
.

Take
c_{m,18},c_{m,19}
such that, for
C_{\gamma,*}>c_{m,19}
, the function
\hat{\chi}
(
4.16
) with
R
satisfying
|R-1|\leq\frac{c_{m,16}}{2C_{\gamma,*}^{3/2}}<1/100
does not have poles on
\gamma_{2}
for
\operatorname{Re}\tau_{c}\leq\operatorname{Re}\tau\leq\operatorname{Re}\tau_{c}+c_{m,18}^{-1}\varepsilon^{2/3}
and below
\gamma_{2}
.

From now on, we fix a constant
C_{\gamma,*}>c_{m,19}
. This fixes also
C_{\gamma},C_{q}
.

Denote by
D_{1,1}
the curvilinear trapezoid bounded by the lines
\operatorname{Re}t=\operatorname{Re}\tau_{c}/\varepsilon,\operatorname{Re}t=\operatorname{Re}\tau_{c}/\varepsilon+c_{m,18}^{-1}/\varepsilon^{1/3},\gamma_{2}
and by the upper boundary of
D_{q,r}
. Let
\bar{D}_{1,1}
be its complex conjugate curvilinear trapezoid.

Describe the domain in complex time to which we should continue the solution (Figure
8
).

Figure 8:
Domains
D_{1},D_{2}
and
D_{3}=D_{3,1}\cup D_{3,2}

This domain is a union of three domains,
D_{1},D_{2},D_{3}
. Domain
D_{1}
is a trapezoid bounded by the lines
\operatorname{Re}t=\operatorname{Re}\tau_{c}/\varepsilon,\operatorname{Re}t=\operatorname{Re}\tau_{c}/\varepsilon+c_{m,18}^{-1}/\varepsilon^{1/3},\gamma_{2},\bar{\gamma_{2}}
.
Domain
D_{2}
is bounded by the curve
\operatorname{Re}\psi_{a}=-C_{a,0}\varepsilon
, by the complex conjugate to it curve, the line
\operatorname{Re}t=\operatorname{Re}\tau_{c}/\varepsilon+c_{m,18}^{-1}/\varepsilon^{1/3}
, and the line
\operatorname{Re}t=\operatorname{Re}\tau_{c}/\varepsilon+C_{a,1}/\varepsilon^{1/3}
. Here
C_{a,0}
and
C_{a,1}
are constants that will be specified later. Complex phase
\psi_{a}
is introduced in (
4.2
). Denote by
P_{1}
the upper right corner of the domain
D_{2}
. Domain
D_{3}
is bounded by the line
\operatorname{Re}t=\operatorname{Re}\tau_{c}/\varepsilon+C_{a,1}/\varepsilon^{1/3}
and by the segment of the curve
\operatorname{Re}\Psi_{\varepsilon}={\rm const}
from
P_{1}
till the real axis (denote
\tau_{+,C_{a,0},\varepsilon}
the corresponding point of the real axis), and the complex conjugate to it curve.
Denote by
Q_{1}
the point of intersection of the curve
\Gamma_{q,r,d}
and the real axis in the
\tau
-plane.
Note that for
d_{+}\sim 1
the vertical distance between the upper boundary of the domain
D_{q,r}
(curve

\Gamma_{q,\varepsilon}
) and the considered level curve
\operatorname{Re}\Psi_{\varepsilon}={\rm const}
is of order
\varepsilon
, while the vertical
width of the domain
\tilde{D}_{q,r,d}
is of order
\varepsilon|\ln\varepsilon|
. Thus, for
d_{+}\sim 1
, the considered level curve
\operatorname{Re}\Psi_{\varepsilon}={\rm const}
is inside the domain
\tilde{D}_{q,r,d}
and passes above
Q_{1}
. We represent the domain
D_{3}
as a union of two domains,
D_{3}=D_{3,1}\cup D_{3,2}
.
The border between
D_{3,1}
and
D_{3,2}
is the vertical line
\operatorname{Re}\tau=\operatorname{Re}Q_{1}
. Note that
\tau_{+,C_{a,0},\varepsilon}-\operatorname{Re}Q_{1}=O(\varepsilon|\ln\varepsilon|)
.

Define the constant
c_{m,22}
as follows. Consider again the expansion (
4.20
):

\hat{\chi}=\sqrt{-\hat{\sigma}}\Bigl[1-2e^{-2iv}e^{\pi i/6}\frac{R-e^{-2\pi i/3}}{R-e^{2\pi i/3}}+O\Bigl(e^{-4|{\rm Im\,}v|}+\frac{1}{|v|}\Bigr)\Bigr].

(7.13)

Here
\hat{\chi},\ \hat{\sigma}
can be replaced with
\chi=\varepsilon^{1/3}\hat{\chi},\ \sigma=\varepsilon^{2/3}\hat{\sigma}
. We have

v=\frac{2}{3}(-\hat{\sigma})^{3/2}=\frac{2}{3}(-\sigma)^{3/2}/\varepsilon
.

For
|R-1|<1/100
, we have

|\frac{R-e^{-2\pi i/3}}{R-e^{2\pi i/3}}|>c_{m,20}^{-1}.

We choose
c_{m,21},c_{m,22}
such that on the curve
\varepsilon^{-1}\operatorname{Re}\psi_{a}=\operatorname{Re}(-2iv)=2\operatorname{Im}v=-C_{a,0}
, for
C_{a,0}>c_{m,21}
, we have in (
7.13
)

|2e^{-2iv}e^{\pi i/6}\frac{R-e^{-2\pi i/3}}{R-e^{2\pi i/3}}+O\Bigl(e^{-4|{\rm Im\,}v|}\Bigr)|>c_{m,22}^{-1}e^{-C_{a,0}}.

(7.14)

We take
P_{1}
on the curve
\varepsilon^{-1}\operatorname{Re}\psi_{a}=-C_{a,0}
in such a way that for
|R-1|<1/100
at
P_{1}
we have in (
7.13
)

|\Bigl[-2e^{-2iv}e^{\pi i/6}\frac{R-e^{-2\pi i/3}}{R-e^{2\pi i/3}}+O\Bigl(e^{-4|{\rm Im\,}v|}+\frac{1}{|v|}\Bigr)\Bigr]|>0.5c_{m,22}^{-1}e^{-C_{a,0}}.

(7.15)

This is guaranteed, provided that
|v|\geq c_{m,23}e^{C_{a,0}}
at
P_{1}
.
At the point
P_{1}
we have

c_{m,24}^{-1}e^{-C_{a,0}}<|\frac{\chi-\sqrt{-\sigma}}{\sqrt{-\sigma}}|<c_{m,24}e^{-C_{a,0}}.

(7.16)

Now we can determine
C_{a,0}
. Constant
C_{a,0}>c_{m,21}
is such that the curve

\varepsilon^{-1}\operatorname{Re}\psi_{a}=-C_{0}
with
\operatorname{Re}\tau_{c}\leq\operatorname{Re}\tau\leq\operatorname{Re}\tau_{c}/\varepsilon+c_{m,18}^{-1}\varepsilon^{2/3}
belongs to
D_{q}
, and is at a distance larger than 1 (in
\tau/\varepsilon^{2/3}
) from the boundary of
D_{q}
.

This guarantees that, for the choice of
C_{a,1}
indicated below, the arc of the curve
\varepsilon^{-1}\operatorname{Re}\Psi_{a}=-C_{a,0}
up to point
P_{1}
belongs to
D_{q}
and stays a positive distance from its boundary.
The choice of
C_{a,1}
should be such that
C_{a,1}>c_{m,25}e^{2C_{a,0}/3}
.

7.5
Third part of the principal part of motion

Lemma 7.6

The solution
x(t),\kappa(t)
can be analytically continued into
D_{1}
with the following estimates.

If
\tau\in D_{1,1}
, then

\displaystyle

\displaystyle|\kappa(t)-\kappa_{c}|=O(\varepsilon^{2/3}),\ |z_{sm}(t)|=O(\varepsilon^{1/3}),\ |w_{sm}(t)|=O(\varepsilon^{2/3}),\ |\eta_{sm}(t)|=O(\varepsilon^{2/3}),

(7.17)

\displaystyle\kappa(t)=\overline{\kappa(\bar{t})},\ z_{sm}(t)=\overline{w_{ms}(\bar{t})},\ w_{sm}(t)=\overline{z_{ms}(\bar{t})},\ \eta_{sm}(t)=\overline{\eta_{ms}(\bar{t})}.

The estimates for
\tau\in D_{q,r}

are given by Lemma
6.8
.

If
\tau\in\bar{D}_{1,1}
, then

\displaystyle

\displaystyle|\kappa(t)-\bar{\kappa}_{c}|=O(\varepsilon^{2/3}),\ |z_{ms}(t)|=O(\varepsilon^{2/3}),\ |w_{ms}(t)|=O(\varepsilon^{1/3}),\ |\eta_{ms}(t)|=O(\varepsilon^{2/3}).

(7.18)

On the part of the curve
\varepsilon^{-1}\operatorname{Re}\Psi_{a}=-C_{a,0}
with
\operatorname{Re}\tau_{c}\leq\operatorname{Re}\tau\leq\operatorname{Re}\tau_{c}+c_{m,18}^{-1}\varepsilon^{2/3}
, we have

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{\varepsilon}(\tau/\varepsilon)=\hat{\sigma}+O(\varepsilon^{1/3}\ln\varepsilon),\ R_{\varepsilon}^{+}(\tau/\varepsilon)=R_{\varepsilon}^{+}(\tau_{c}/\varepsilon)+O(\varepsilon^{1/3}),

(7.19)

\displaystyle\hat{\chi}_{\varepsilon}(\tau/\varepsilon)=-i\sqrt{-\hat{\sigma}}\,\frac{J_{-2/3}(v)-R^{+}_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{c}/\varepsilon)J_{2/3}(v)}{R^{+}_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{c}/\varepsilon)J_{-1/3}(v)+J_{1/3}(v))}+O(\varepsilon^{1/3}\ln\varepsilon),\ v=\frac{2}{3}(-\hat{\sigma})^{3/2},

\displaystyle\hat{\chi}_{\varepsilon}(\tau/\varepsilon)=\sqrt{-\hat{\sigma}}\Bigl[1-2e^{-2iv}e^{\pi i/6}\frac{R^{+}_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{c}/\varepsilon)-e^{-2\pi i/3}}{R^{+}_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{c}/\varepsilon)-e^{2\pi i/3}}+O\Bigl(e^{-4|{\rm Im\,}v|}+\frac{1}{|v|}\Bigr)\Bigr]+O(\varepsilon^{1/3}\ln\varepsilon),

\displaystyle c_{m,26}^{-1}e^{-C_{a,0}}-c_{m,27}\frac{1}{|v|}+O(\varepsilon^{1/3}\ln\varepsilon)<|\frac{\hat{\chi}_{\varepsilon}(\tau/\varepsilon)-\sqrt{-\hat{\sigma}}}{\sqrt{-\hat{\sigma}}}|<c_{m,26}e^{-C_{a,0}}+c_{m,27}\frac{1}{|v|}+O(\varepsilon^{1/3}\ln\varepsilon),

\displaystyle|\kappa(t)-\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon t)|=O(\varepsilon).

Note that
\kappa
in the text of this Lemma denotes the original variable
\kappa
, while in the text of Lemma
6.8
it denotes the variable transformed as described in Lemmas
5.3
and
5.4
.

7.6
Fourth part of the principal part of motion

Lemma 7.7

For
C_{a,1}>c_{m,25}e^{2C_{a,0}/3}
,
the solution
z(t),\eta(t),\kappa(t)
can be analytically continued into the domain
D_{2}
with the estimates given in Lemma
6.8
. At the point
P_{1}
we have

c_{m,28}^{-1}\varepsilon^{1/3}\sqrt{|\hat{\sigma}|}e^{-C_{a,0}}<|z|<c_{m,28}\varepsilon^{1/3}\sqrt{|\hat{\sigma}|}e^{-C_{a,0}}.

(7.20)

7.7
Fifths part of the principal part of motion

Denote by
\Gamma^{C_{a,0},\varepsilon}
the arc of the curve
\operatorname{Re}\Psi_{\varepsilon}={\rm const}
passing through the point
P_{1}
.

Lemma 7.8

Arc
\Gamma^{C_{a,0},\varepsilon}
crosses the real axis
\operatorname{Im}\tau=0
at a point
\tau_{+,C_{a,0},\varepsilon}=\tau_{*}^{+}+O(\varepsilon\ln\varepsilon)
.

Denote by
\hat{\sigma}_{1}
and
z_{1}
the values of
\hat{\sigma}
and
z
at the point
P_{1}
. We have

\hat{\sigma}_{1}=c_{m,29}C_{a,1}
,
c_{m,28}^{-1}\varepsilon^{1/3}\sqrt{|\hat{\sigma}_{1}|}e^{-C_{a,0}}<|z_{1}|<c_{m,28}\varepsilon^{1/3}\sqrt{|\hat{\sigma}_{1}|}e^{-C_{a,0}}
.

Lemma 7.9

For
C_{a,1}>{\rm max}\{c_{m,25}e^{2C_{a,0}/3},c_{m,30}e^{C_{a,0}/3}\}
,
the solution
z(t),\eta(t),\kappa(t)
can be analytically continued into the domain
D_{3,1}
with estimates given in Lemmas
6.8
and
6.9
.
On the curve
\Gamma^{C_{a,0},\varepsilon}
we have

0.5|z_{1}|<|z|<2|z_{1}|,\ \eta=O(\varepsilon),\ w=O(\varepsilon).

Lemma 7.10

The solution
z(t),\eta(t),\kappa(t)
can be analytically continued into the domain
D_{3,2}
, and at
\tau=\tau_{+,C_{a,0,}\varepsilon}
we have
|z(t)|=|w(t)|>c_{m,31}^{-1}\varepsilon^{1/3},\eta(t)=O(\varepsilon),|\kappa(t)-\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon t)|=O(\varepsilon)
.

The variables
z,w,\eta,\kappa
here are those obtained after transformations in Lemma
5.4
. Now we should return to the original variables.

Lemma 7.11

At
\tau=\tau_{+,C_{a,0},\varepsilon}
in the the original variables

z,w,\eta,\kappa
we have
|z(t)|=|w(t)|>c_{m,32}^{-1}\varepsilon^{1/3},\eta(t)=O(\varepsilon^{2/3}),|\kappa(t)-\mathrm{K}(\varepsilon t)|=O(\varepsilon)
.

Lemma 7.12

There exists a real
\tau_{d}=\tau^{+}_{*}+O(\varepsilon\ln\varepsilon)
such that
|x(t_{d})-X(\kappa(t_{d}))|>c_{m,33}^{-1}
.

This completes the proof of Theorem
1
.

8
Proofs of lemmas about curves

Proof of Lemma
4.1
.

For definiteness, consider the case
j=1
. The proof for
j=2
is analogous. Let
Oxy
be the orthogonal coordinate frame in the complex slow time plane such that the point
O
is at
\tau_{c}
, and the axis
Ox
is directed along
\gamma_{1}
(Figure
9
).

Figure 9:
For lemmas
4.1
,
4.2

Let
\rho,\theta
be corresponding polar coordinates:
x=\rho\cos\theta,y=\rho\sin\theta
. Then
\Psi(\tau)-\Psi(\tau_{c})=i\alpha(x+iy)^{3/2}+O(\rho^{2})=i\alpha\rho^{3/2}(\cos(3\theta/2)+i\sin(3\theta/2))+O(\rho^{2})
. Here
\alpha
is a positive constant. The equation for

\Gamma_{*,1}
is
\operatorname{Re}(\Psi(\tau)-\Psi(\tau_{c}))=0
, which implies
-\alpha\rho^{3/2}\sin(3\theta/2))+O(\rho^{2})=0
. Thus,

\sin(3\theta/2))+O(\rho^{1/2})=0
,
and
\theta=O(\rho^{1/2})
. The distance between points of
\gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{*,1}

measured along normals to
\gamma_{1}

is
y=\rho\sin\theta=O(\rho^{3/2})=O(|\tau-\tau_{c}|^{3/2})
. The same estimate for the distance remains valid if this distance is measured along normals to
\Gamma_{*,1}
and
\tau
is taken as the parameter along
\Gamma_{*,1}
. This is because the angle between the tangent line to
\Gamma_{*,1}
and the
Ox
-axis is
O(\rho^{1/2})
.

\square

Proof of Lemma
4.2
.

Let
x,y,\rho,\theta
be variables introduced in the proof of Lemma
4.1
(Figure
9
).
Denote

\Phi(x,y)=\operatorname{Re}(\Psi(\tau)-\Psi(\tau_{c}))
. Equations of curves

\Gamma_{*,1}
and
\Gamma_{\tau_{-}}
are, respectively,
\Phi(x,y)=0
and
\Phi(x,y)=a_{-}
with
|a_{-}|\sim|\tau_{-}-\tau_{*}^{-}|
. We have
\Phi(x,y)=-\alpha\rho^{3/2}\sin(3\theta/2))+O(\rho^{2})
(see proof of Lemma
4.1
). Here
\alpha
is a positive constant. For small
x,y,\rho,\theta
, when
x\geq 0
, this can be rewritten as

\Phi(x,y)=-\frac{3}{2}\alpha(x^{2}+y^{2})^{1/4}y+O((x^{2}+y^{2}))
, and one can write equations for
\Gamma_{*,1}
and
\Gamma_{\tau_{-}}
as, respectively,
y=y_{*,1}(x)
and
y=y_{-}(x)
. We have

\frac{\partial\Phi(x,y_{*})}{\partial y}(y_{-}-y_{*,1})=a_{-},

where
y_{*}
is a value between
y_{-}
and
y_{*,1}
. For
|y|<k_{1}^{-1}x
, the principal part of
{\partial\Phi(x,y)}/{\partial y}
is

-\frac{3}{2}\alpha\left((x^{2}+y^{2})^{1/4}-\frac{y^{2}}{2(x^{2}+y^{2})^{3/4}}\right)=-\frac{3}{2}\alpha\frac{2x^{2}-y^{2}}{2(x^{2}+y^{2})^{3/4}}\sim x^{1/2}.

Thus,

y_{-}-y_{*,1}\sim\frac{a_{-}}{x^{1/2}}\sim\frac{|\tau_{-}-\tau_{*}^{-}|}{\sqrt{|\tau-\tau_{c}|}}.

Here
\tau
is considered as a parameter along the
Ox
-axis, i.e. along
\gamma_{1}
, and the distance is measured along normals to

\gamma_{1}
. The same estimate is valid if
\tau
is used as the parameter along along
\Gamma_{*,1}
, and the distance is measured along normals to
\Gamma_{*,1}
.

The condition
|y|<k_{1}^{-1}x
is satisfied provided that
|\tau_{-}-\tau_{*}^{-}|/\sqrt{|\tau-\tau_{c}|}<k_{2}^{-1}{|\tau-\tau_{c}|}
, i.e.
|\tau-\tau_{c}|>k_{2}^{2/3}|\tau_{-}-\tau_{*}^{-}|^{2/3}
.

\square

Proof of Lemma
4.3
.

For
|\tau-\tau_{c}|\sim|\tau_{q}-\tau_{c}|
the statement of the Lemma is evident, because in this case the distance between points of
\Gamma_{q}
and
\Gamma_{*,1}\cup\Gamma_{*,2}
is of order
|\tau_{q}-\tau_{c}|
. Thus, one should only consider the case

|\tau-\tau_{c}|>>|\tau_{q}-\tau_{c}|
. For this case, the proof is completely analogous to the proof of Lemma
4.2
with
|a_{-}|\sim|\tau_{q}-\tau_{c}|^{3/2}
.

\square

9
Proofs of lemmas about Riccati equation.

Proof of lemma
4.4
.

Substitute into (
4.7
) the asymptotic formulas for Bessel functions with large
|v|
. For real
v
, we obtain

\displaystyle\hat{\zeta}

\displaystyle=-i\sqrt{-\hat{s}}\,\frac{\cos(v+\pi/12)-R\cos(v-7\pi/12)}{R\cos(v-\pi/12)+\cos(v-5\pi/12)}(1+O(|v|^{-1}))

\displaystyle=-i\sqrt{-\hat{s}}\,\frac{\cos(v+\pi/12)-R\sin(v-\pi/12)}{R\cos(v-\pi/12)+\sin(v+\pi/12)}(1+O(|v|^{-1})).

We would like to find
R
such that

\frac{\cos(v+\pi/12)-R\sin(v-\pi/12)}{R\cos(v-\pi/12)+\sin(v+\pi/12)}=-i.

(9.1)

This relation gives

\cos(v+\pi/12)-R\sin(v-\pi/12)=-iR\cos(v-\pi/12)-i\sin(v+\pi/12).

Thus,

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle=\frac{\cos(v+\pi/12)+i\sin(v+\pi/12)}{-i[\cos(v-\pi/12)+i\sin(v-\pi/12)]}=e^{i\pi/2}\frac{e^{i\pi/12}}{e^{-i\pi/12}}=e^{i\pi/2}e^{i\pi/6}=e^{i2\pi/3}.

\square

Proof of lemma
4.5
.

We have

\displaystyle\hat{\zeta}

\displaystyle=-i\sqrt{-\hat{s}}\,\frac{J_{-2/3}(v)+O(|v|^{2/3})}{RJ_{-1/3}(v)+O(|v|^{1/3})}=-i\sqrt{-\hat{s}}\,\frac{(v/2)^{-2/3}/\Gamma(1/3)+O(|v|^{2/3})}{R(v/2)^{-1/3}/\Gamma(2/3)+O(|v|^{1/3})}

\displaystyle=-i\sqrt{-\hat{s}}\,\frac{1}{R}\left(\frac{(v/2)^{-1/3}\Gamma(2/3)}{\Gamma(1/3)}+O(|v|^{1/3})\right)=-i\sqrt{-\hat{s}}\,\frac{1}{R}\left(\frac{(-s)^{3/2}/3)^{-1/3}\Gamma(2/3)}{\Gamma(1/3)}+O(|v|^{1/3})\right)

\displaystyle=-i\,\frac{1}{R}\left(\frac{3^{1/3}\Gamma(2/3)}{\Gamma(1/3)}+O(|v|^{2/3})\right).

Using the reflection formula for the Gamma function

\Gamma(\nu)\Gamma(1-\nu)=\frac{\pi}{\sin{\pi\nu}}

with
\nu=1/3
, we obtain

\displaystyle\hat{\zeta}

\displaystyle=\,\frac{1}{R}\frac{-2\pi i}{\Gamma^{2}(1/3)3^{1/6}}(1+O(|v|^{2/3})).

\square

Proof of lemma
4.6
.

This proof is analogous to the proof of Lemma
4.4
, but one should replace
i
with
-i
in the right hand side of equality (
9.1
). Then we obtain
R=e^{-i2\pi/3}
.

\square

Proof of lemma
4.7
.

The values
\zeta
and
\chi
at
\tau=\tau_{c}
for the same solution of equation
4.3
are related by

e^{-i\pi/6}\zeta=e^{-i5\pi/6}\chi
, which implies
\chi=e^{i2\pi/3}\zeta
. On the other hand, Lemma
4.5
implies that, for
\tau=\tau_{c}
,

\zeta=\frac{k}{R_{l}},\quad\chi=\frac{k}{R_{r}}

with the same constant
k
. Hence,
R_{r}=e^{-i2\pi/3}R_{l}
.

\square

Proof of lemma
4.8
.

Substituting into (
4.16
) the asymptotic formulas for Bessel functions with large
|v|
, we obtain

\displaystyle\hat{\chi}

\displaystyle=-i\sqrt{-\hat{\sigma}}\,\frac{\cos(v+\pi/12)-R\cos(v-7\pi/12)}{R\cos(v-\pi/12)+\cos(v-5\pi/12)}(1+O(|v|^{-1}))

\displaystyle=-i\sqrt{-\hat{\sigma}}\,\frac{e^{i(v+\pi/12)}+e^{-i(v+\pi/12)}-R(e^{i(v-7\pi/12)}+e^{-i(v-7\pi/12)})}{R(e^{i(v-\pi/12)}+e^{-i(v-\pi/12)})+e^{i(v-5\pi/12)}+e^{-i(v-5\pi/12)}}(1+O(|v|^{-1}))

\displaystyle=-i\sqrt{-\hat{\sigma}}\,\frac{e^{i\pi/12}-Re^{-i7\pi/12}+e^{-2iv-i\pi/12}-Re^{-2iv+i7\pi/12}}{Re^{-i\pi/12}+e^{-i5\pi/12}+Re^{-2iv+\pi/12}+e^{-2iv+i5\pi/12}}(1+O(|v|^{-1})

\displaystyle=i\sqrt{-\hat{\sigma}}\,\frac{Re^{-i7\pi/12}-e^{i\pi/12}-e^{-2iv-i\pi/12}+Re^{-2iv+i7\pi/12}}{Re^{-i\pi/12}+e^{-i5\pi/12}+Re^{-2iv+\pi/12}+e^{-2iv+i5\pi/12}}(1+O(|v|^{-1}))

\displaystyle=i\sqrt{-\hat{\sigma}}\,\frac{e^{-i7\pi/12}[R-e^{i8\pi/12}-e^{-2iv+i6\pi/12}+Re^{-2iv+14i\pi/12}]}{e^{-i\pi/12}[R+e^{-i4\pi/12}+Re^{-2iv+2\pi/12}+e^{-2iv+6i\pi/12}]}(1+O(|v|^{-1}))

\displaystyle=i\sqrt{-\hat{\sigma}}\,e^{-i\pi/2}\frac{R-e^{i2\pi/3}+e^{-2iv}[-e^{i\pi/2}-Re^{i\pi/6}]}{R-e^{i2\pi/3}+e^{-2iv}[Re^{i\pi/6}+e^{i\pi/2}]}(1+O(|v|^{-1}))

\displaystyle=\sqrt{-\hat{\sigma}}\left(1+e^{-2iv}\frac{2e^{-i\pi/2}-2Re^{i\pi/6}}{R-e^{i2\pi/3}}+O(e^{-4|\operatorname{Im}v|}\right)(1+O(|v|^{-1}))

\displaystyle=\sqrt{-\hat{\sigma}}\left(1-2e^{-2iv}e^{i\pi/6}\frac{R-e^{-i2\pi/3}}{R-e^{i2\pi/3}}+O(e^{-4|\operatorname{Im}v|}+|v|^{-1})\right).

\square

Proof of lemma
4.9
.

We have

\frac{\partial\hat{\zeta}}{\partial R}=i\sqrt{-\hat{s}}\ \frac{J_{2/3}(v)J_{1/3}(v)+J_{-2/3}(v)J_{-1/3}(v)}{(RJ_{-1/3}(v)+J_{1/3}(v))^{2}},\quad v=\frac{2}{3}(-\hat{s})^{3/2}.

(9.2)

According to
[
13
]
, Sec. 7.11, formula (33),

J_{\nu}(v)J_{-\nu+1}(v)+J_{-\nu}(v)J_{\nu-1}(v)=\frac{2\sin(\nu\pi)}{\pi v}.

For
\nu=1/3
, we obtain

J_{1/3}(v)J_{2/3}(v)+J_{-1/3}(v)J_{-2/3}(v)=\frac{\sqrt{3}}{\pi v}=\frac{3\sqrt{3}}{2\pi(-\hat{s})^{3/2}}

Thus,

\frac{\partial\hat{\zeta}}{\partial R}=-i\frac{3\sqrt{3}}{2\pi\hat{s}}\frac{1}{(RJ_{-1/3}(v)+J_{1/3}(v))^{2}}

\square

10
Proofs of lemmas about transformation of variables

Proof of Lemma
4.12
.

For definiteness, we give the proof for the domain
V^{+}_{\delta}
. Recall notation:

\displaystyle K^{+}_{*,\delta}=\{\tau\ :\ \tau\in K_{*}^{+},\ \operatorname{Im}\tau<\operatorname{Im}\tau_{c}-\delta\},\ \delta=C_{\delta}\varepsilon^{2/3},

\displaystyle B_{*,{\delta}}^{+}=\{\kappa\ :\ \kappa=\mathrm{K}(\tau),\tau\in K^{+}_{*,\delta}\},

\displaystyle d_{+}=d_{+}(\kappa)=|b\cdot(\kappa-\kappa_{c})|,

V_{{\delta}}^{+}
is the
c_{l,4}^{-1}\varepsilon^{2/3}
-neighbourhood of
B_{*,{\delta}}^{+}
. The equilibrium
X(\kappa)
of the fast system is well defined and is an analytic function of
\kappa
in
V_{{\delta}}^{+}
.

We should prove that for
sufficiently large
c_{l,4}
we have
c_{l,6}^{-1}d_{+}<|\kappa-\kappa_{c}|<c_{l,6}d_{+}
in
V_{\delta}^{+}
.

Take any
\kappa_{v}\in V_{\delta}^{+}
. Let
\kappa_{b}
be the point of
B_{*,{\delta}}^{+}
closest to
\kappa_{v}
. Denote

d_{+,v}=d_{+}(\kappa_{v}),d_{+,b}=d_{+}(\kappa_{b})
.
Then

|\kappa_{v}-\kappa_{b}|\leq c_{l,4}^{-1}\varepsilon^{2/3},\quad|d_{+,v}-d_{+,b}|\leq k_{1}c_{l,4}^{-1}\varepsilon^{2/3}.

Let us first derive the required estimates for
\kappa_{b}
. The domain
B_{*,{\delta}}^{+}
is parametrised by values of the slow time
\tau
from
K^{+}_{*,\delta}
. For

\kappa_{b}
,
we have
\kappa_{b}=\mathrm{K}(\tau),\tau\in K^{+}_{*,\delta}
. Thus, for such
\kappa_{b}
, we have

\displaystyle\kappa_{b}-\kappa_{c}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{K}(\tau)-\mathrm{K}(\tau_{c})=\frac{d\mathrm{K}(\tau_{i})}{d\tau}(\tau-\tau_{c})=g(X(\mathrm{K}(\tau_{i})),\tau_{i},0)(\tau-\tau_{c})

\displaystyle=g(x_{c},\tau_{c},0)(\tau-\tau_{c})+O(|\tau-\tau_{c}|^{3/2}),

\displaystyle b\cdot(\kappa_{b}-\kappa_{c})

\displaystyle=b\cdot(\mathrm{K}(\tau)-\mathrm{K}(\tau_{c}))=b\cdot\frac{d\mathrm{K}(\tau_{i})}{d\tau}(\tau-\tau_{c})=b\cdot g(X(\mathrm{K}(\tau_{i})),\tau_{i},0)(\tau-\tau_{c})

\displaystyle=b\cdot g(x_{c},\kappa_{c},0)(\tau-\tau_{c})+O(|\tau-\tau_{c}|^{3/2}),

where
\tau_{i}
is a point on the segment joining
\tau
and
\tau_{c}
. We have

d_{+,b}=|b\cdot(\kappa_{b}-\kappa_{c})|=|b\cdot g(x_{c},\kappa_{c},0)||\tau-\tau_{c}|+O(|\tau-\tau_{c}|^{3/2}).

According to assumption G in Section
2
,
|b\cdot g(x_{c},\kappa_{c},0)|>k_{2}^{-1}
. Then

k_{3}^{-1}|\tau-\tau_{c}|<|\kappa_{b}-\kappa_{c}|<k_{3}|\tau-\tau_{c}|,\ k_{4}^{-1}d_{+,b}<|\tau-\tau_{c}|<k_{4}d_{+,b},\ k_{5}^{-1}d_{+,b}<|\kappa_{b}-\kappa_{c}|<k_{5}d_{+,b}.

This, in particular, implies that

|\kappa_{b}-\kappa_{c}|>k_{3}^{-1}|\tau-\tau_{c}|>k_{3}^{-1}\delta=k_{3}^{-1}C_{\delta}\varepsilon^{2/3},\quad d_{+,b}>k_{4}^{-1}|\tau-\tau_{c}|>k_{4}^{-1}\delta=k_{4}^{-1}C_{\delta}\varepsilon^{2/3}.

Then, for sufficiently large constant
c_{l,4}
, we have

|\kappa_{v}-\kappa_{b}|<0.5|\kappa_{b}-\kappa_{c}|,\quad|d_{+,v}-d_{+,b}|<0.5d_{+,b}

which implies

(0.5)^{2}k_{5}^{-1}d_{+,v}<0.5|\kappa_{b}-\kappa_{c}|<|\kappa_{v}-\kappa_{c}|<1.5|\kappa_{b}-\kappa_{c}|<(1.5)^{2}k_{5}d_{+,v}.

Thus,

c_{l,6}^{-1}d_{+}(\kappa)<|\kappa-\kappa_{c}|<c_{l,6}d_{+}(\kappa)

in
V_{\delta}^{+}
for a sufficiently large constant
c_{l,6}
.

\square

Proof of Lemma
5.1
.

For definiteness, we give the proof for the domain
W^{+}_{\delta}
. Substitute
x=\xi+X(\kappa)
into (
1.1
). We obtain

\displaystyle\frac{d\xi}{dt}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(\xi+X(\kappa),\kappa,0)-\varepsilon\frac{\partial X(\kappa)}{\partial\kappa}g(\xi+X(\kappa),\kappa,0)+O(\varepsilon),

(10.1)

\displaystyle\frac{d\kappa}{dt}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon g(\xi+X(\kappa),\kappa,0)+O(\varepsilon^{2}).

Equation (
2.1
) and conditions F, G of Section
2
imply that
X(\kappa)
has a singularity of type
(b\cdot(\kappa-\kappa_{c}))^{1/2}
. Thus

\partial X(\kappa)/\partial\kappa=O^{*}(d_{+}^{-1/2})
. This implies estimates of part (a) of the lemma.

For the part (b) of the lemma, consider first equation (
1.1
) in variables
z_{1},z_{2}
of (
2.1
). We obtain

\displaystyle\dot{z}_{1}=b\cdot(\kappa-\kappa_{c})+az_{1}^{2}+O(\varepsilon+|z_{1}|^{3}+|\kappa-\kappa_{c}|(|z_{1}|+|z_{2}|)+|z_{1}||z_{2}|+|z_{2}|^{2}+|\kappa-\kappa_{c}|^{2}),

(10.2)

\displaystyle\dot{z}_{2}={\cal A}_{c,2}\,z_{2}+b_{1}\cdot(\kappa-\kappa_{c})+O(\varepsilon+|z_{1}|^{2}+|\kappa-\kappa_{c}|(|z_{1}|+|z_{2}|)+|z_{1}||z_{2}|+|z_{2}|^{2}+|\kappa-\kappa_{c}|^{2}),

where
{\cal A}_{c,2}
is a non-degenerate matrix,
b_{1}={\rm const}
. To find the equilibrium, we solve equation
\dot{z}_{2}=0
for
z_{2}
first. This determines
z_{2}
as an analytic function of
z_{1},\kappa
, whose expansion with respect to
z_{1},\kappa-\kappa_{c}
starts with terms proportional to
z_{1}^{2}
and to
\kappa-\kappa_{c}
. Substitute this function into the equation
\dot{z}_{1}=0
and solve it for
z_{1}
. The resulting expression for
z_{1}
as a function of
\kappa
has a square-root singularity of the form
(b\cdot(\kappa-\kappa_{c}))^{1/2}=(\tilde{d}_{+}(\kappa))^{1/2}
. Substitute this value of
z_{1}
into already obtained solution of the equation
\dot{z}_{2}=0
. This implies the estimates in part (b) of the Lemma.

Proof of Lemma
5.2
.

For definiteness, we give the proof for the domain
W^{+}_{\delta}
. Condition 4) is Section
2
implies that
\lambda_{1}(\kappa)\neq\lambda_{2}(\kappa)
,
\lambda_{i}(\kappa)\neq\lambda_{j}(\kappa),\quad i=1,2,\quad j=3,\ldots,n
for
\kappa\in V^{+}_{\delta}
. This allows to choose a real-analytic basis which is a union of bases in eigen-spaces of the matrix
{\cal A}
, corresponding eigen-values
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
and
\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4},\ldots,\lambda_{n}
. This basis can first be constructed locally; its definition can then be extended globally by propagating it along solutions of the slow system.
Details of this construction are given in
[
20
]
, pp.282 - 285. The matrix
C
provides the reduction of the matrix
{\cal A}
to this basis. The matrix
C
is non-degenerate and depends analytically on the position of the equilibrium
X(\kappa)
, for which we have

\partial X(\kappa)/\partial\kappa=O^{*}(d_{+}^{-1/2})
.
Substitute
\xi=C(\kappa)\tilde{\xi}
into (
1.1
). Differentiation of
C(\kappa)
over time leads to terms
\varepsilon O^{*}(d_{+}^{-1/2})\xi
and
\varepsilon O(d_{+}^{-1/2}|\xi|^{2})
in equations.
The second of these terms is absorbed into the term
O(|\xi|^{2})
in (
5.2
).

Proof of Lemma
5.3
.

For definiteness, we give the proof for the domain
W^{+}_{\delta}
. Let
\xi=(z,w,\eta)
.

(a) We make a shift of the coordinate origin
\xi\mapsto\tilde{\xi}
so that the new origin coincides with the equilibrium point of the equation for new fast variables with frozen
\kappa
. We compute this shift in three steps. First, we solve the equations
\dot{w}=0,\dot{\eta}=0
for
w,\eta
and substitute the obtained solution into the equation
\dot{z}=0
. Second, we solve the resulting equation
\dot{z}=0
for
z
. Third, we substitute this value of
z
into the already obtained solution of the equations
\dot{w}=0,\dot{\eta}=0
.
This procedure yields an estimate of the shift of
z
of order

O^{*}(\varepsilon d_{+}^{-1})
, and of the other fast variables of order

O^{*}(\varepsilon)+O^{*}(\varepsilon^{2}d_{+}^{-3/2})+O^{*}(\varepsilon^{2}d_{+}^{-2})
=
O^{*}(\varepsilon)+O^{*}(\varepsilon^{2}d_{+}^{-2})=O^{*}(\varepsilon d_{+}^{-1/2})
. The term
O^{*}(\varepsilon^{2}d_{+}^{-3/2})
appears due to the term
O^{*}(\varepsilon d_{+}^{-1/2}\xi)
in (
5.2
), while the term
O^{*}(\varepsilon^{2}d_{+}^{-2})
appears due to
\sim z^{2}
terms in the equations
\dot{w}=0,\dot{\eta}=0
. Note that an additional linear term
O^{*}(\varepsilon d_{+}^{-1})\tilde{\xi}
appears in the equations for new fast variables. This is due to the shift in terms
\sim z^{2}
. There are also new nonlinear terms

O(\varepsilon d_{+}^{-1}|\tilde{\xi}|^{2})
. These terms are absorbed in the term
O(|\tilde{\xi}|^{2})
.

The considered shift in
\xi
leads to change of form of the equation for
\dot{\kappa}
. An additional
\tilde{\xi}
-independent term
O^{*}(\varepsilon^{2}d_{+}^{-1})
appears due to the shift in the term
\sim z
. Also, an additional linear in
\tilde{\xi}
term
O^{*}(\varepsilon^{2}d_{+}^{-1}|\tilde{\xi}|)
and nonlinear in
\tilde{\xi}
term
O(\varepsilon^{2}d_{+}^{-1}|\tilde{\xi}|^{2})
appear there. These terms are absorbed in the terms
O^{*}(\varepsilon|\tilde{\xi}|)
and
O(\varepsilon|\tilde{\xi}|^{2})
.

Additional new terms appear in the right hand sides of the differential equations for the new variables due to the time dependence of
\kappa
. The terms that do not vanish at
\tilde{\xi}=0
are
O^{*}(\varepsilon^{2}d_{+}^{-2})
for
\dot{\tilde{z}}
and
O^{*}(\varepsilon^{2}d_{+}^{-1/2})+O^{*}(\varepsilon^{3}d_{+}^{-3})=O^{*}(\varepsilon^{2}d_{+}^{-3/2})
for other fast variables. Linear terms in
\dot{\kappa}
produce a linear term
O^{*}(\varepsilon^{2}d_{+}^{-2})\tilde{\xi}=O^{*}(\varepsilon d_{+}^{-1})\tilde{\xi}
in
\dot{\tilde{\xi}}
. Nonlinear terms in
\dot{\kappa}
produce a nonlinear term
O(\varepsilon^{2}d_{+}^{-2}|\tilde{\xi}|^{2})
in

\dot{\tilde{\xi}}
. This term is absorbed in the term
O(\varepsilon|\tilde{\xi}|^{2})
.

Repeat the same step in the new variables.
Now the shift is
O^{*}(\varepsilon^{2}d_{+}^{-5/2})
for the new variable
z
and
O^{*}(\varepsilon^{2}d_{+}^{-1/2})+O^{*}(\varepsilon^{3}d_{+}^{-3})+O^{*}(\varepsilon^{4}d_{+}^{-5})=O^{*}(\varepsilon^{2}d_{+}^{-2})
for other fast variables. In the equation for the new variables the term that does not vanish at
\tilde{\xi}=0
is
O^{*}(\varepsilon^{3}d_{+}^{-7/2})
for
\dot{\tilde{z}}
and
O^{*}(\varepsilon^{3}d_{+}^{-3})
for other fast variables.

(b) The matrix of the linearised near
\xi=0
system differs from a block-diagonal form by
O^{*}(\varepsilon d_{+}^{-1})
. We perform a linear transformation of variables that reduces this matrix to block-diagonal form for fixed
\kappa
. The matrix of this transformation differs from the unit matrix by
O^{*}(\varepsilon d_{+}^{-1})
. As a result, we obtain a system in which the matrix of the linearised near
\xi=0
system differs from a block-diagonal form by
O^{*}(\varepsilon^{2}d_{+}^{-2})
. One further similar step reduces the deviation from block-diagonality to
O^{*}(\varepsilon^{3}d_{+}^{-3})
.
Thus, the equation for
\xi
has the form of (
5.3
) with the block diagonal matrix
A
.

Proof of Lemma
5.4
.

We use the standard procedure from normal form theory for the elimination of non-resonant terms (see, e.g.,
[
1
]
, Ch. 5). We start with the proof for the domain
W^{+}_{\delta}
.

First, we perform a standard real-analytic transformation of variables

\eta,z,w
which, for frozen
\kappa
, eliminates from the right-hand side of the equation for

\dot{\eta}

the monomials
z^{2},w^{2},z^{3},w^{3}
;
from the equation for
\dot{z}
the monomial
w^{2}
;
and from the equation for
\dot{w}
the monomial
z^{2}
.
For this transformation we have the estimate

\hat{\xi}=\xi+O(|\xi|^{2})
(‘‘hat’’ for new variables). New quadratic and higher order terms appear in the transformed equations due to dependence of the transformation on
\kappa
. These terms are proportional to
\varepsilon
. The estimate for these terms is
\varepsilon O(|\xi|^{2}d_{+}^{-1/2})
.

Then, we perform a standard real-analytic transformation of variables
z,w

which, for frozen
\kappa
, eliminates from
the equation for
\dot{z}
all quadratic monomials containing
z
, and from equation for
\dot{w}
all quadratic monomials containing
w
.
This transformation satisfies the estimates

\displaystyle\hat{z}=z+O(|z|^{2}d_{+}^{-1/2}+|\xi|^{2}),\ \hat{w}=w+O(|zw|d_{+}^{-1/2}+|\xi|^{2})

(‘‘hats’’ for new variables).
New quadratic and higher order terms appear in the transformed equations due to the dependence of the transformation on
\kappa
. These terms are proportional to
\varepsilon
. The estimates for these terms are
\varepsilon O(|z|^{2}d_{+}^{-3/2})+\varepsilon O(|\xi|_{*}d_{+}^{-3/2})
in the equation for
\dot{z}
, and
\varepsilon O(|zw|d_{+}^{-3/2})+\varepsilon O(|\xi|^{2}d_{+}^{-1/2})
in the equation for
\dot{w}

(we omit “hats” over
the new variables).

The transformation under consideration leads to the appearance of new third- and higher-order terms (not proportional to
\varepsilon
) due to the substitution of the formulas for the new variables into the right-hand sides of the equations.
In equation for
\dot{z}
, the terms not proportional to
z^{3}
are estimated by
O(|\xi|_{*}^{3}d_{+}^{-1/2})
. The terms proportional to
z^{3}
are estimated by
z^{3}O^{*}(d_{+}^{-1/2})+z^{4}O^{*}(d_{+}^{-1/2})
and
z^{4}O^{*}(d_{+}^{-1})+z^{5}O(d_{+}^{-1/2})
(these terms originate from the terms
\sim z^{2}
and
\sim z^{3}
in the original equation, respectively). The term
z^{3}O^{*}(d_{+}^{-1/2})
can be eliminated by a transformation of variables. The new large term created by this is
z^{5}O^{*}(d_{+}^{-3/2})
. The terms
z^{4}O^{*}(d_{+}^{-1/2})
and
z^{4}O^{*}(d_{+}^{-1})
we also eliminate by a transformation of variables. The new large term created by this is
z^{7}O(d_{+}^{-5/2})
. This term is majorated by
z^{5}O(d_{+}^{-3/2})
. Other terms generated by these transformations are smaller than those already present in the equation. To preserve real analyticity, we perform a similar transformation in the equation for

\dot{w}
replacing
z
with
w
.
In the equation for
\dot{\eta}
the higher order terms are
O(|\xi|_{*}^{3}d_{+}^{-1/2})+O(|z|^{4})
.
In the equation for
\dot{w}
the higher order terms are
O(|\xi|_{*}^{3}d_{+}^{-1/2})+O(|z|^{3})
.

Combining the above estimates, we obtain the formulas for
\dot{z},\dot{\eta}
in (
5.7
).

In the domain
W^{-}_{\delta}
, the nearly-resonant quadratic monomial in the expression for
\dot{z}
is
zw
. The transformation of
z
is estimated by

\displaystyle\hat{z}=z+O(|zw|d_{-}^{-1/2}+|\xi|^{2}).

The dependence of this transformation on
\kappa
gives terms
\varepsilon O^{*}(|zw|d_{-}^{-3/2}+|\xi|^{2}d_{-}^{-1/2})
(‘‘hats’’ are omitted). This transformation creates some new cubic terms. The cubic and higher order terms are bounded above by
O(|\xi|_{**}^{3}d_{-}^{-1/2})
+
O(|w|^{3})
.
Hence, we obtain (
13.8
).

Proof of Lemma
5.5
.

For definiteness, we give the proof for the domain
W^{+}_{\delta}
.

We start by working on the terms linear in
\xi
in the equation for
\dot{\kappa}
. To this end, we make the transformation of variables

\hat{\kappa}=\kappa+\varepsilon M(\kappa,\varepsilon)\xi,

(10.3)

where
M
is a matrix to be determined. The new variable
\hat{\kappa}
satisfies the equation

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{\kappa}}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon G(\kappa)+O^{*}(\varepsilon^{2}d_{+}^{-1})+\varepsilon E(\kappa)\xi+\varepsilon O(|\xi|^{2})

\displaystyle+\varepsilon M(\kappa,\varepsilon)A(\kappa,\varepsilon)\xi+\varepsilon M\alpha+\varepsilon\dot{M}(\kappa,\varepsilon)\xi,

where
A(\kappa,\varepsilon)
is the matrix of the linearised near
\xi=0
system,
E(\kappa)=O(1
),
O(|\xi|^{2})
is the sum of nonlinear terms in
\dot{\kappa}
, and
\alpha
denotes all terms in
\dot{\xi}
except
A\xi
.

Solving the equation
M(\kappa,\varepsilon)A(\kappa,\varepsilon)+E(\kappa)=0
for
M
, we obtain
M=-A^{-1}E
. In the variables
z,w,\eta
, the matrix
A
has a block diagonal structure. Thus,
A^{-1}
has the block diagonal structure, where the first
1\times 1
block is
O(d_{+}^{-1/2})
, while entries in all other blocks are
O(1)
. Note that derivatives in
\kappa
of these blocks are
O(d_{+}^{-3/2})
and
O(d_{+}^{-1/2})
, respectively. Using expressions for
\dot{z},\dot{\eta}
in (
5.7
), and information about
\dot{w}
in Lemma
5.4
, we obtain

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{\kappa}}=\varepsilon G(\kappa)+O^{*}(\varepsilon^{2}d_{+}^{-1})+\varepsilon O^{*}(|\xi|^{2})+\varepsilon O^{*}(|\xi|^{3})+\varepsilon O(|\xi|^{4})

\displaystyle+\varepsilon d_{+}^{-1/2}O\left(\varepsilon|z|^{2}d_{+}^{-3/2}+\varepsilon(|\xi|_{*}^{2}d_{+}^{-1/2})+O(|\eta|(|\eta|+|w|))+(|\xi|_{*}^{3}d_{+}^{-1/2})+(|z|^{5}d_{+}^{-3/2})+\varepsilon^{3}(d_{+}^{-3}|\xi|)\right)

\displaystyle+O^{*}(\varepsilon d_{+}^{-1/2}\varepsilon^{3}(d_{+}^{-7/2}))

\displaystyle+\varepsilon O\left(|\eta|^{2}+|z\eta|+\varepsilon((|z|^{2}+|w|^{2}+|w\eta|)d_{+}^{-1/2})+(\varepsilon|wz|d_{+}^{-3/2})+(|\xi|^{3}_{*}d_{+}^{-1/2})+(|z|^{3})+\varepsilon^{3}(d_{+}^{-3}|\xi|)\right)

\displaystyle+O^{*}(\varepsilon\varepsilon^{3}(d_{+}^{-3}))

\displaystyle+\varepsilon O\left(|\eta|^{2}+(|\eta|(|z|+|w|)+O(|zw|)+\varepsilon((|z|^{2}+|w|^{2})d_{+}^{-1/2})+(|\xi|_{*}^{3}d_{+}^{-1/2})+(|z|^{4})+\varepsilon^{3}(d_{+}^{-3}|\xi|)\right)

\displaystyle+O^{*}(\varepsilon\varepsilon^{3}(d_{+}^{-3}))

\displaystyle+\varepsilon^{2}O(|z|d_{+}^{-3/2}+(|w|+|\eta|)d_{+}^{-1/2}).

The substitution of
\hat{\kappa}
from (
10.3
) into
G(\kappa)
creates additional linear in
\xi
terms

\varepsilon^{2}O(d_{+}^{-1}|z|+d_{+}^{-1/2}|\xi|_{*})
.
Comparing the magnitudes of the different terms, we obtain the following form of this equation (‘‘hat’’ over
\kappa
is omitted):

\displaystyle\dot{\kappa}=\varepsilon G(\kappa)+O^{*}(\varepsilon^{2}d_{+}^{-1})+\varepsilon O\left(|z|^{2}+d_{+}^{-1/2}|\xi|_{*}^{2}\right)+\varepsilon d_{+}^{-1}O(|\xi|_{*}^{3})+\varepsilon O^{*}(|z|^{3})

\displaystyle+\varepsilon^{2}O(|z|^{2}d_{+}^{-2})+\varepsilon O\left(|z|^{4}+|z|^{5}d_{+}^{-2}\right)+\varepsilon^{2}O\left(|z|d_{+}^{-3/2}+d_{+}^{-1/2}(|w|+|\eta|)\right)+\varepsilon^{4}O(d_{+}^{-7/2}|\xi|).

Repeat similar step for the new linear in
\xi
terms in the equation. We obtain the equation in which linear in
\xi
term is

\varepsilon^{3}O(d_{+}^{-3}|z|+d_{+}^{-3/2}(|w|+|\eta|))
. The estimates for the other terms do not change. This is because the new additional terms arising from
\dot{\xi}
are the same as those from the previous step, but multiplied by
O(\varepsilon d_{+}^{-3/2})=O(1)
.

In a similar way, we can eliminate the quadratic in
\xi
terms
\varepsilon O^{*}\left(|z|^{2}+d_{+}^{-1/2}|\xi|_{*}^{2}\right)
and the cubic in
z
term
\varepsilon O^{*}(|z|^{3})
. The estimate for the new quadratic and cubic terms is
\varepsilon^{2}O\left(d_{+}^{-3/2}|z|^{2}+d_{+}^{-3/2}|\xi|_{*}^{2}\right)
. The estimates for the other terms do not change. This is because the new additional terms arising from
\dot{\xi}
are the same as those on the previous step, but multiplied by
O(d_{+}^{-1/2}|\xi|)=O(1)
.

Comparing the magnitudes of the different terms, we obtain (
5.6
).

11
Proofs of lemmas about continuation of solutions.

Proof of lemma
6.1
.

The differential equations for
\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}
and
\mathrm{K}
in the domain
V_{\delta}^{\pm}
are

\dot{\mathrm{K}}_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon F(\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}),\quad\dot{\mathrm{K}}=\varepsilon G(\mathrm{K}),\quad F(\kappa)=G(\kappa)+\varepsilon O(d_{\pm}^{-1}).

(11.1)

For the initial condition of
\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}
, we have

\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{*}^{-})=\mathrm{K}(\tau_{*}^{-})+O(\varepsilon).

In
V_{\delta}^{\pm}
, we have
|\partial G/\partial\kappa|=O(d_{\pm}^{-1/2})
,
\hat{d}_{\pm}(\tau)>k_{1}^{-1}\varepsilon^{2/3}
. The estimates for solutions of (
11.1
) give

|\mathrm{K}(\tau)-\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\tau)|=O(\varepsilon(1+|\ln\hat{d}_{\pm}|)),\quad 0.5\hat{d}_{\pm}(\tau)<\hat{d}_{\pm,\varepsilon}(\tau)<2\hat{d}_{\pm}(\tau)

in
V_{\delta}
.

\square

Proof of lemmas
6.2
,
6.3
,
6.4
.

We provide the proof of Lemma
6.2
. Proofs of other lemmas are completely analogous.

We have

\operatorname{Re}\int_{\tau_{\gamma}}^{\tau_{*,\varepsilon,-}}\Lambda_{1}(\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\vartheta))\,d\vartheta=0,\quad\operatorname{Re}\int_{\tau_{\gamma}}^{\tau_{-}}\lambda_{1}(\mathrm{K}(\vartheta))\,d\vartheta=0

and

\Lambda_{1}(\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\tau))=\lambda_{1}(\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\tau))+\varepsilon O(\hat{d}_{+,\varepsilon}^{-1})=\lambda_{1}(\mathrm{K}(\tau))+\varepsilon|\ln\hat{d}_{+}|O(\hat{d}_{+}^{-1/2})+\varepsilon O(\hat{d}_{+}^{-1}).

This implies

\operatorname{Re}\int_{\tau_{*,\varepsilon,-}}^{\tau_{-}}\lambda_{1}(\mathrm{K}(\vartheta))\,d\vartheta=\varepsilon\operatorname{Re}\int_{\tau_{\gamma}}^{\tau_{*,\varepsilon,-}}\left(|\ln\hat{d}_{+}|O(\hat{d}_{+}^{-1/2})+O(\hat{d}_{+}^{-1})\right)d\vartheta=O(\varepsilon\ln\varepsilon).

This implies
\tau_{*,\varepsilon,-}=\tau_{-}+O(\varepsilon\ln\varepsilon)
.

\square

Proof of lemmas
6.5
,
6.6
.

We will give a detailed proof of Lemma
6.6
. At the end of this proof we will make a comment concerning modifications required for the proof of Lemma
6.5
.

Denote by
O_{1},O_{2},O_{3},O_{4}
last
O
-terms in equations (
5.7
), (
5.8
). We have

|O_{1}({d}_{+}^{-7/2})|<c_{r,1}{d}_{+}^{-7/2},\ |O_{2}({d}_{+}^{-3})|<c_{r,2}{d}_{+}^{-3},\ |O_{3}({d}_{+}^{-1})|<c_{r,3}{d}_{+}^{-1},\ |O_{4}({d}_{-}^{-3})|<c_{r,4}{d}_{-}^{-3}.

(11.2)

Rewrite system (
5.7
) in the domain
W^{+}_{\delta}
in the form

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\dot{z}=\Lambda_{1}(\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon})z+(\Lambda_{1}(\kappa)-\Lambda_{1}(\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}))z+\beta_{1}+\varepsilon^{3}O_{1}(d_{+}^{-7/2}),

(11.3)

\displaystyle\dot{\eta}=B(\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon})\eta+(B(\kappa)-B(\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}))\eta+\beta_{2}+\varepsilon^{3}O_{2}(d_{+}^{-3}),

\displaystyle\dot{\kappa}=\varepsilon F(\kappa)+\varepsilon^{2}O(|z|^{2}d_{+}^{-2})+\varepsilon^{2}O(|\xi|_{*}^{2}d_{+}^{-3/2})

\displaystyle+\varepsilon O\left(|z|^{4}+|z|^{5}d_{+}^{-2}+|\xi|_{*}^{3}\right)+\varepsilon^{3}O(|z|d_{+}^{-3}+|\xi|_{*}d_{+}^{-3/2})+\varepsilon^{4}O(d_{+}^{-7/2}|\xi|),

\displaystyle F=g(X(\kappa),\kappa,0)+\varepsilon O_{3}(d_{+}^{-1}).

In the domain
W^{-}_{\delta}
, we have similar equations with
z
and
w
interchanged and with
d_{+}
replaced by
d_{-}
, and
\Lambda_{1}
replaced by

\Lambda_{2}
.

Rewrite equation for
z
in the domain
W^{-}_{\delta}
in the form

\displaystyle\dot{z}=\Lambda_{1}(\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon})z+(\Lambda_{1}(\kappa)-\Lambda_{1}(\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}))z+\beta_{4}+\varepsilon^{3}O_{4}(d_{-}^{-3}).

(11.4)

We have

|\kappa(t_{4})-\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon t_{4})|=O(\varepsilon^{6}|\ln\varepsilon|),\ |z(t_{4})|=O(\varepsilon^{3}),\ |\eta(t_{4})|=O(\varepsilon^{3}).

(11.5)

Denote by
S(T)
the part of the domain
D_{q,l}
(the sector) in the plane of the complex time where

t_{4}\leq\operatorname{Re}t\leq T
. We consider the solution
z(t),\eta(t),\kappa(t)
of system (
5.7
), (
5.8
).
By Cauchy’s theorem
[
12
]
, there exists
T>t_{4}
such that the solution
z(t),\eta(t),\kappa(t)
can be analytically continued into the sector
S(T)
.

Take
T
such that for
t\in S(T)
we have:

\displaystyle

\displaystyle|\beta_{1}|<0.5\varepsilon^{3}c_{r,1}{d}_{+}^{-7/2},\ |\beta_{2}|<0.5\varepsilon^{3}c_{r,2}{d}_{+}^{-3},\ |\beta_{4}|<0.5\varepsilon^{3}c_{r,4}{d}_{-}^{-3},

(11.6)

\displaystyle 0.5\hat{d}_{\pm}(\varepsilon t)\leq d_{\pm}(\kappa(t))\leq 2\hat{d}_{\pm}(\varepsilon t),

\displaystyle|\kappa(t)-\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(t)|<\mu_{1}\varepsilon^{4}\hat{d}_{\pm}^{-9/2},

\displaystyle|\xi(t)|<c_{t,5}\varepsilon^{1/3}

(‘‘+’’ and ‘‘-’’ correspond respectively for
\operatorname{Im}\tau\geq 0
and
\operatorname{Im}\tau\leq 0
). Here,

is a positive constant whose value does not depend on choice of the constant
C_{q}
, provided that
C_{q}
is sufficiently large. The value
\mu_{1}
is determined after the statement of Lemma
11.1
. The constant
c_{t,5}
is introduced in the statement of Lemma
5.4
.

Note that inequalities (
11.6
) are certainly satisfied for sufficiently small
T-t_{4}
.

Assumptions (
11.6
) imply that

\displaystyle|\Lambda_{1}(\kappa(t))-\Lambda_{1}(\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon t))||z|=\mu_{1}\varepsilon^{4}O(\hat{d}_{\pm}^{-5})\varepsilon^{1/3}<0.1c_{r,1}\varepsilon^{3}\hat{d}_{\pm}^{-7/2},

\displaystyle|B(\kappa(t))-B(\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon t))||\eta|=\mu_{1}\varepsilon^{4}O(\hat{d}_{\pm}^{-5})\varepsilon^{1/3}<0.1c_{r,2}\varepsilon^{3}\hat{d}_{\pm}^{-3}.

(Here we use that
|\xi|<c_{t,5}\varepsilon^{1/3}
and that

is sufficiently large.)

Thus, equations (

) have the form

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\dot{z}=\Lambda_{1}(\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon})z+\varepsilon^{3}\tilde{O}_{1}(d_{+}^{-7/2}),

(11.7)

\displaystyle\dot{\eta}=B(\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon})\eta+\varepsilon^{3}\tilde{O}_{2}(d_{+}^{-3})

with
|\tilde{O}_{1}(d_{+}^{-7/2})|<2c_{r,1}d_{+}^{-7/2},\ |\tilde{O}_{2}(d_{+}^{-3})|<2c_{r,2}d_{+}^{-3}
.

Equation (

) has the form

\displaystyle\dot{z}=\Lambda_{1}(\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon})z+\varepsilon^{3}\tilde{O}_{4}(d_{-}^{-3})

(11.8)

with
|\tilde{O}_{4}(d_{-}^{3})|<2c_{r,4}d_{-}^{-3}
.

Lemma 11.1

In
S(T)
, for
\operatorname{Im}\tau\geq-c_{l,1}^{-1}
we have

\displaystyle

\displaystyle|z(t)|<c_{r,5}\varepsilon^{2}{d}_{+}^{-5/2},\ |\eta(t)|<c_{r,6}\varepsilon^{3}{d}_{+}^{-3},

(11.9)

\displaystyle|\kappa(t)-\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon t)|<c_{r,7}\varepsilon^{4}\hat{d}_{+}^{-9/2};

for
\operatorname{Im}\tau\leq c_{l,1}^{-1}
we have

\displaystyle

\displaystyle|z(t)|<c_{r,6}\varepsilon^{3}{d}_{-}^{-3},\ |\eta(t)|<c_{r,6}\varepsilon^{3}{d}_{-}^{-3},

(11.10)

\displaystyle|\kappa(t)-\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon t)|<c_{r,7}\varepsilon^{4}\hat{d}_{-}^{-9/2}

The constants
c_{r,5},c_{r,6},c_{r,7}
do not depend on the value
T
or on the choice of the constant
\mu_{1}
.

We take
\mu_{1}=2c_{r,7}
.

Lemma 11.2

If the constant
c_{e,5}
is chosen sufficiently large, then for any
\varepsilon T<\operatorname{Re}\tau_{c}
the assumptions (
11.6
) are satisfied with a margin.

Thus, one can take
\varepsilon T=\operatorname{Re}\tau_{c}
. Then, the estimates in Lemma
11.1
imply the estimates in Lemma
6.6
.

The proof of Lemma
6.5
is completely analogous. Just Lemma
11.1
should be replaced with an analogous lemma for the domain
D_{p}
. Namely, in the statement of Lemma
11.1
, one should replace
D_{q},C_{q}
by
D_{\gamma},C_{\gamma}
, or
D_{p},C_{p}
. It should be noted that the constants in the
estimates can be chosen uniformly with respect to the choice of
\tau_{p}
.

\square
.

Proof of lemma
6.7
.

Consider downward motion along the vertical line
\operatorname{Re}\tau=\operatorname{Re}\tau_{c}
from the point
\tau=\tau_{q}
.
For this motion, the equation for
z
in (
5.7
) takes the form

\frac{dz}{ds}=-i\Lambda_{1}(\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon t))z+\varepsilon^{3}O(\hat{d}_{+}^{-7/2}),\ s=-\operatorname{Im}t,

(11.11)

where
-i\Lambda_{1}(\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon t))
is real and negative, bounded above by
-c_{a,1}^{-1}\hat{d}_{+}^{1/2}
.
For
|z|
, we obtain a differential inequality

\frac{d|z|}{ds}<-c_{a,1}^{-1}\hat{d}_{+}^{1/2}|z|+c_{a,2}\varepsilon^{3}\hat{d}_{+}^{-7/2}.\

(11.12)

Consider a linear differential equation for a real variable
u
:

\frac{du}{ds}=-c_{a,1}^{-1}\hat{d}_{+}^{1/2}u+c_{a,2}\varepsilon^{3}\hat{d}_{+}^{-7/2}.

(11.13)

Denote by
s_{0}
the value of
s
corresponding to
\tau=\tau_{q}
. Note that

|z(\tau_{q}/\varepsilon)|<c_{e,7}\varepsilon^{2}\hat{d}_{+}^{-5/2}(\tau_{q})<c_{a,3}\varepsilon^{1/3}C_{q}^{-5/2}
.

Denote by
u(s)
the solution of (
11.13
) with the initial condition
u(s_{0})=c_{a,3}\varepsilon^{1/3}C_{q}^{-5/2}
. According to Lemma
17.2
,
for
s_{0}\leq s\leq s_{0}+c_{a,4}^{-1}/\varepsilon
, we have

|z(t)|<u(s)
.

Denote
\hat{d}_{q}=\hat{d}_{+}(\tau_{q})
. Choose
s_{1}
such that the change
in
\hat{d}_{+}
on the interval
[s_{0},s_{1}]
does not exceed
\hat{d}_{q}/2
. We may take
s_{1}-s_{0}=c_{a,5}^{-1}/\varepsilon
with
c_{a,5}>c_{a,4}
. Consider the linear differential equation with constant coefficients

\frac{dv}{ds}=-\nu v+\alpha,\ \nu=c_{a,1}^{-1}2^{-1/2}\hat{d}_{q}^{1/2},\ \alpha=c_{a,2}(3/2)^{7/2}\varepsilon^{3}\hat{d}_{q}^{-7/2}.

(11.14)

Denote by
v(s)
the solution of this equation with the initial condition
v(s_{0})=u(s_{0})
. For
s_{0}<s\leq s_{1}
we have
u(s)<v(s)
.

According to Lemma
17.1
, for

\displaystyle s\geq s_{0}+\frac{1}{\nu}\left|\ln\left(\frac{\nu v(s_{0})}{\alpha}\right)\right|=s_{0}+\frac{1}{c_{a,1}^{-1}2^{-1/2}\hat{d}_{q}^{1/2}}\left|\ln\left(\frac{c_{a,1}^{-1}2^{-1/2}\hat{d}_{q}^{1/2}c_{e,7}\varepsilon^{2}\hat{d}_{q}^{-5/2}}{c_{a,2}(3/2)^{7/2}\varepsilon^{3}\hat{d}_{q}^{-7/2}}\right)\right|

(11.15)

\displaystyle=s_{0}+c_{a,8}\hat{d}_{q}^{-1/2}|\ln(c_{a,7}^{-1}\varepsilon\hat{d}_{q}^{-3/2})|

we have

v(s)<2\frac{\alpha}{\nu}=2\frac{c_{a,2}(3/2)^{7/2}\varepsilon^{3}\hat{d}_{q}^{-7/2}}{c_{a,1}^{-1}2^{-1/2}\hat{d}_{q}^{1/2}}=c_{a,6}\varepsilon^{3}\hat{d}_{q}^{-4}.

We have

c_{a,8}\hat{d}_{q}^{-1/2}|\ln(c_{a,7}^{-1}\varepsilon\hat{d}_{q}^{-3/2})|<c_{a,9}\varepsilon^{-1/3}C_{q}^{-1/2}(\ln C_{q}).

Denote
s_{2}=s_{0}+c_{a,9}\varepsilon^{-1/3}C_{q}^{-1/2}(\ln C_{q})
. For
s_{2}\leq s\leq s_{1}
we have
0.5\hat{d}_{q}<\hat{d}_{+}<1.5\hat{d}_{q}
.
Thus,
|z(t)|\leq u(s)\leq v(s)<c_{a,6}\varepsilon^{3}\hat{d}_{q}^{-4}<c_{a,10}\varepsilon^{3}\hat{d}_{+}^{-4}
for such values of
s
.
Thus, we obtain the result of the Lemma with
c_{e,9,1}=c_{a,9},\ c_{e,9,2}=c_{a,5},c_{e,9,3}=c_{a,10}
.

\square

Proof of lemma

if
